Appendix A
Proofs

A.1
Proof of Theorem
1

The fact that (
2
) imply
(
3
) and (
4
)
follows from Assumption
0\in S
, which implies that

\pi=\mathbb{P}(X>0)\in(0,1)
and
1-\pi=\mathbb{P}(X<0)\in(0,1)
, and convergence
of types
(
Billingsley 2008
, Theorem 1.4.2 in)
, with normalizing
sequences satisfying

\displaystyle b_{n,\{+1\}}\sim(b_{n}-m)+[(\pi^{-\xi_{\{+1\}}}-1)/\xi_{\{+1\}}]a_{n}

and

\displaystyle a_{n,\{+1\}}\sim a_{n}\pi^{-\xi_{\{+1\}}},

\displaystyle b_{n,\{-1\}}\sim(\beta_{n}-m)+[((1-\pi)^{-\xi_{\{-1\}}}-1)/\xi_{\{-1\}}]\alpha_{n}

and

\displaystyle a_{n,\{-1\}}\sim\alpha_{n}(1-\pi)^{-\xi_{\{-1\}}}

as
n\to\infty
. To show that the sequence of mean measures in
expression (
5
) converges vaguely in

M_{+}((\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{-1\}}}\times\{-1\})\cup(\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{+1\}}}\times\{+1\})\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
, we need to
show that for any

h\in C_{K}^{+}((\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{-1\}}}\times\{-1\})\cup(\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{+1\}}}\times\{+1\})\color[rgb]{0,0,0})

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{(\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{-1\}}}\times\{-1\})\cup(\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{+1\}}}\times\{+1\})}h(z,w)n\mathbb{P}\left[\left(\frac{|X|-r_{n}^{a}(X/|X|)}{r_{n}^{b}(X/|X|)},X/|X|\right)\in dz\times dw\right]

(A.1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{(\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{-1\}}}\times\{-1\})\cup(\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{+1\}}}\times\{+1\})}h(z,w)\Lambda(dz\times dw),

with
\Lambda
defined in expression
(
6
). Note that because

(\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{-1\}}}\times\{-1\})\cup(\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{+1\}}}\times\{+1\})
is a disjoint union,
then
h(z,w)=h_{\{-1\}}(z)\mathbbm{1}_{\{w=-1\}}+h_{\{+1\}}(z)\mathbbm{1}_{\{w=1\}}
,
with
h_{\{-1\}}\in C_{K}^{+}(\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{-1\}}})
and

h_{\{+1\}}\in C_{K}^{+}(\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{+1\}}})
. Using total
probability, the integral in expression (
A.1
)
equals

\displaystyle(1-\pi)

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{-1\}}}}h_{\{-1\}}(z)n\mathbb{P}\left[\left(\frac{|X|-r_{n}^{a}(X/|X|)}{r_{n}^{b}(X/|X|)}\in dz~\Big|~X/|X|=-1\right)\right]+

\displaystyle+\pi\int_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{+1\}}}}h_{\{+1\}}(z)n\mathbb{P}\left[\left(\frac{|X|-r_{n}^{a}(X/|X|)}{r_{n}^{b}(X/|X|)}\in dz~\Big|~X/|X|=+1\right)\right].

Convergences (
3
) and
(
4
) guarantee that as
n\to\infty
, the
latter expression converges to

\displaystyle(1-\pi)\int_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{-1\}}}}h_{\{-1\}}\,d\nu_{\{-1\}}+\pi\int_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{+1\}}}}h_{\{+1\}}\,d\nu_{\{+1\}}=\int_{(\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{-1\}}}\times\{-1\})\cup(\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{\{+1\}}}\times\{+1\})}h\,d\Lambda,

completing the proof.

A.2
Convergence in distribution of renormalised sample minimum
and maximum

By replacing
1
with
\pi+(1-\pi)
in
1-\mathbb{P}_{n}:=\overline{\mathbb{P}}_{n}

and by applying the law of total probability to
\mathbb{P}_{n}
, we average
the conditional probabilities given
\{X\geq 0\}
and
\{X<0\}
over

\pi
and
1-\pi
and note that, due to conditions (
3
) and (
4
),
which guarantee that both
a_{n,\{+1\}}z_{+}+b_{n,\{+1\}}
and

a_{n,\{-1\}}(-z_{-})+b_{n,\{-1\}}
are ultimately positive, we
find that

\displaystyle n\overline{\mathbb{P}}_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\pi)n\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{|X|-b_{n,\{-1\}}}{a_{n,\{-1\}}}>-z_{-}~\Big|X<0\right)+\pi n\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{|X|-b_{n,\{+1\}}}{a_{n,\{+1\}}}>z_{+}~\Big|X>0\right),

(A.2)

for all sufficiently large

n
. Thus, the convergences (
3
) and
(
4
) lead to

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{m_{n}+b_{n,\{-1\}}}{a_{n,\{-1\}}}>z_{-},\frac{M_{n}-b_{n,\{+1\}}}{a_{n,\{+1\}}}<z_{+}\right)=G_{0}(-z_{-},z_{+}\mid\pi),\quad\text{as $n\to\infty$},

(A.3)

at continuity points of the limit distribution

G_{0}
given by expression (
10
).

A.3
Convergence to Poisson point process

Proof of Theorem
2
.

To prove the weak convergence of the random point measure to the
stated limit Poisson random measure, we need to show that for any

h\in C_{K}^{+}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cup_{\bm{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi(\bm{w})}\times\{\bm{w}\}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})

\displaystyle\nu_{n}(h):=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi(\bm{w})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}h(z,\bm{w})\,\nu_{n,\bm{w}}(dz)\mathbb{P}_{\bm{X}/\lVert\bm{X}\rVert}(d\bm{w})\to\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi(\bm{w})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}h(z,\bm{w})\,\nu_{\bm{w}}(dz)\mathbb{P}_{\bm{X}/\lVert\bm{X}\rVert}(d\bm{w})=:\nu(h),

as
n\to\infty
. It suffices to show
that
\lvert\nu_{n}(h)-\nu(h)\rvert\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Since

h
is continuous on a compact set, say
K_{h}\subset\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{d}
, we can
find a compact cover

\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times[r_{\inf},r_{\sup}]\supset K_{h}
, with

r_{\inf}=\inf\{\lVert\bm{x}\rVert\,:\,\bm{x}\in K_{h}\}
and

r_{\sup}=\sup\{\lVert\bm{x}\rVert\,:\,\bm{x}\in K_{h}\}
, such
that

\displaystyle\lvert\nu_{n}(h)-\nu(h)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\lVert h\rVert_{\infty}(\bm{w})\lvert\mu_{n,\bm{w}}\rvert([r_{\inf},r_{\sup}])\mathbb{P}_{\bm{X}/\lVert\bm{X}\rVert}(\bm{w}),

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\lVert h\rVert_{\infty}(\bm{w})\Delta(\bm{w})\mathbb{P}_{\bm{X}/\lVert\bm{X}\rVert}(d\bm{w})\quad\text{for
all $n>n_{0}$},

where

\lVert h\rVert_{\infty}(\bm{w})=\sup\{h(z,\bm{w})\,:\,z\in\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi(\bm{w})}\}<\infty
. Since

\lim_{n\to\infty}(\nu_{n,\bm{w}}(h)-\nu_{\bm{w}}(h))=0,

dominated convergence gives that

\lvert\nu_{n}(h)-\nu(h)\rvert\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
∎

A.4
Properties of probability sets

Proof of Proposition
5
.

(i)
Using total probability and the change of the
integrating measure
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}
to
\mu
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{W}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\text{supp}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{X}/\lVert\bm{X}\rVert})}\mathbb{P}(R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{w})\mid\bm{W}=\bm{w})\,\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}}{d\mu}(\bm{w})\,\mu(d\bm{w})

Multiplication and division by
1/(1-q)
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{W}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-q)\int_{\text{supp}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}})}\frac{\mathbb{P}(R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{w})\mid\bm{W}=\bm{w})}{1-q}\,\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}}{d\mu}(\bm{w})\,\mu(d\bm{w}).

From the definition (
28
) of

r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{w})
, we have that for any

q>q_{l}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu^{\star})
,

\frac{\mathbb{P}(R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{w})\mid\bm{W}=\bm{w})}{1-q}=\left(\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}}{d\mu}(\bm{w})\right)^{-1}.

Thus, since
\mu
is a probability measure on
\text{supp}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}})
,

\mathbb{P}[R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{W})]=(1-q)\int_{\text{supp}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}})}\mu(d\bm{w})=(1-q).

Next, we consider the conditional distribution of
\bm{W}
given

R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{W})
. For any measurable set

B\subset\text{supp}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bm{W}\in B\mid R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{W})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}\in B\right)/\mathbb{P}(R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{W}))

Because
\mathbb{P}[R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{W})]=(1-q)
,
it follows that for any
q>q_{l}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu^{\star})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bm{W}\in B\mid R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{W})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-q)^{-1}\int_{B}\mathbb{P}\left(R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{w})\mid\bm{W}=\bm{w}\right)\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}(d\bm{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-q)^{-1}\int_{B}(1-q)\frac{d\mu}{\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}}(\bm{w})\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}(d\bm{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{B}\mu(\bm{w})=\mu(B),

which completes the proof of
(i)
.

(ii)
For each
z
in some open interval
(a,b)
that is a continuity
point of the limit measure
\nu_{\bm{w}}
, we have by Assumption that

1

\lim_{n\to\infty}n\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{R-b_{n}(\bm{W})}{a_{n}(\bm{W})}>z~\Big|~\bm{W}=\bm{w}\right]\varrho_{\mu}(\bm{w})=[1+\xi(\bm{w})z]_{+}^{-1/\xi(\bm{w})}\varrho_{\mu}(\bm{w}),

(A.4)

for
\bm{w}\in\text{supp}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}})
, where

\varrho_{\mu}=d\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}/d\mu>0
. Convergence
(
A.4
) is equivalent to

\lim f_{n}(z\,;\,\bm{w})=g(z\,;\,\bm{w})
for
\bm{w}\in\text{supp}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}})
,
where

\displaystyle f_{n}(z\,;\,\bm{w})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle 1\Big/\left(n\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{R-b_{n}(\bm{W})}{a_{n}(\bm{W})}>z~\Big|~\bm{W}=\bm{w}\right]\varrho_{\mu}(\bm{w})\right)

(A.5)

\displaystyle g(z\,;\,\bm{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[1+\xi(\bm{w})z]_{+}^{1/\xi(\bm{w})}/\varrho_{\mu}(\bm{w}),

(A.6)

for
\bm{w}\in\text{supp}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}})
. Since a sequence of non-decreasing
functions in
z
converges to a limit function that is
non-decreasing in
z
, Lemma 1.1.1 in
de Haan & Ferreira 2006
applies and
for each
y\in(g(a\,;\,\bm{w}),g(a\,;\,\bm{w}))
that is a
continuity point of
g^{-1}
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}^{-1}(y\,;\,\bm{w})=g^{-1}(y\,;\,\bm{w})
,
where
f_{n}^{-1}
and
g^{-1}
denote the left-continuous inverses of

f_{n}
and
g
, respectively. That is,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{V_{\bm{w}}(ny\,;\,\mu)-b_{n}(\bm{w})}{a_{n}(\bm{w})}=\frac{\{y\varrho_{\mu}(\bm{w})\}^{\xi(\bm{w})}-1}{\xi(\bm{w})},\quad\bm{w}\in\text{supp}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}),

where

V_{\bm{w}}(y\,;\,\mu)=\inf\left\{z\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,\left(n\mathbb{P}(R>z\mid\bm{W}=\bm{w})\varrho_{\mu}(\bm{w})\right)^{-1}\geq y\right\},

is defined for
y>1
. Note that the function

V_{\bm{w}}(n\,;\,\mu)
satisfies

V_{\bm{w}}(1/(1-q)\,;\,\mu)=r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}\,\|\,\mu)}(\bm{w})
.

Since
\inf\{\xi(\bm{w})\,:\,\bm{w}\in\text{supp}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{X}/\lVert\bm{X}\rVert})\}>-1
, it suffices to
show that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{V_{\bm{w}}(ny\,;\,\mu)-V_{\bm{w}}(n\,;\,\mu)}{V_{\bm{w}}(n\{1+\xi(\bm{w})\}^{1/\xi(\bm{w})}\,;\,\mu)-V_{\bm{w}}(n\,;\,\mu)}=\frac{y^{\xi(\bm{w})}-1}{\xi(\bm{w})},\quad\bm{w}\in\text{supp}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}).

(A.7)

The left-hand side of expression (
A.7
) equal

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\cfrac{\cfrac{V_{\bm{w}}(ny\,;\,\mu)-b_{n}(\bm{w})}{a_{n}(\bm{w})}-\cfrac{V_{\bm{w}}(n\,;\,\mu)-b_{n}(\bm{w})}{a_{n}(\bm{w})}}{\cfrac{V_{\bm{w}}(n(1+\xi(\bm{w}))^{1/\xi(\bm{w})}\,;\,\mu)-b_{n}(\bm{w})}{a_{n}(\bm{w})}-\cfrac{V_{\bm{w}}(n\,;\,\mu)-b_{n}(\bm{w})}{a_{n}(\bm{w})}}

\displaystyle=\cfrac{\cfrac{\{y\varrho_{\mu}(\bm{w})\}^{\xi(\bm{w})}-1}{\xi(\bm{w})}-\cfrac{\varrho_{\mu}(\bm{w})^{\xi(\bm{w})}-1}{\xi(\bm{w})}}{\cfrac{\{(1+\xi(\bm{w}))^{1/\xi(\bm{w})}\varrho_{\mu}(\bm{w})\}^{\xi(\bm{w})}-1}{\xi(\bm{w})}-\cfrac{\varrho_{\mu}(\bm{w})^{\xi(\bm{w})}-1}{\xi(\bm{w})}}

\displaystyle=\cfrac{\rho_{\mu}(\bm{w})^{\xi(\bm{w})}(y^{\xi(\bm{w})}-1)/\xi(\bm{w})}{\varrho_{\mu}(\bm{w})^{\xi(\bm{w})}}=\cfrac{y^{\xi(\bm{w})}-1}{\xi(\bm{w})},\qquad\bm{w}\in\text{supp}(\mathbb{P}_{\bm{W}}).

∎

A.5
Density convergence of rescaled radial excess
variable

Below, we use Lemma
1
, a direct
consequence of the Corollary 3.3 of
de Haan & Resnick 1987
.

Lemma 1

(Potter bounds)
.

Let
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and suppose there exist
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,
h:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and

\mathcal{G}\in\bigstar
such that
\lim_{t\to\infty}V(t\bm{x})/h(t)=g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{\rho}
uniformly on

\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Then, for any

\varepsilon>0
, there exists

t_{0}=t_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
t>t_{0}
,

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)\lVert\bm{x}\rVert^{-\varepsilon}g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{\rho}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{V(t\bm{x})}{h(t)}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\lVert\bm{x}\rVert^{\varepsilon}g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{\rho},\qquad\forall\,\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}.

Proof of Proposition
6
.

Consider
the following relation

\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\,\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{R-r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}{r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})}\leq z~\Big|~R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}=\bm{w}\right]=\frac{r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})z_{q}(\bm{w})^{d-1}f_{\bm{X}}(z_{q}(\bm{w})\bm{w})}{\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})}f_{R,\bm{W}}(s,\bm{w})~\mathrm{d}s},

(A.8)

where
z_{q}(\bm{w})=r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})+r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})z
,
z\in[0,\{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})-r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})\}/r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})]
, and
\bm{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Write the integral in expression (
A.8
) using the change of variable
s=\{1-(t\lVert\bm{w}\rVert)^{-1}\}\,r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})
, with
L_{q}(\bm{w}):=r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})/\{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})-r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})\}
, as

\displaystyle\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{w})}}f_{R,\bm{W}}(s,\bm{w})~\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})^{d}\int_{L_{q}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}\left(1-1/t\right)^{d-1}t^{-2}\{f_{\bm{X}}[(1-1/t)r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})\bm{w}]/\psi_{\textsf{B}}(t)\}\psi_{\textsf{B}}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Due to convergence
(i)
, the map

(0,\infty)\ni s\mapsto f_{\bm{X}}[(1-1/s)r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})\bm{w}]/\psi_{\textsf{B}}(s)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is slowly varying at
\infty
. Additionally, convergence
(i)
implies that

\psi_{\mathsf{B}}(s)\in\text{RV}_{1+1/\xi}^{\infty}
, hence
there exists
l_{\textsf{B}}\in\text{RV}_{0}^{\infty}
such that

\psi_{\mathsf{B}}(s)=l_{\mathsf{B}}(s)s^{1+1/\xi}
. Thus, by Karamata’s theorem
(
Resnick 2007
, Theorem
2.1)
,

\frac{\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{w})}}f_{R,\bm{W}}(s,\bm{w})~\mathrm{d}s}{\{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})-r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})\}f_{R,\bm{W}}[r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}),\bm{w}]}=-\xi\{1+o(1)\},\qquad\text{as $q\to 1$}.

Using the assumption that
f_{\bm{X}}(r\bm{w})\sim\psi_{\mathsf{B}}[r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})/\{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})-r\}]g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{1+1/\xi}
as
r\to r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})
, and setting
r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})=-\xi\{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})-r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})\}
, expression (
A.8
) converges to

\displaystyle\frac{r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})z_{q}(\bm{w})^{d-1}f_{\bm{X}}[z_{q}(\bm{w})\bm{w}]}{-\xi\{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})-r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})\}r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})^{d-1}f_{\bm{X}}[r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})\bm{w}]}\{1+o(1)\}=

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{q}(\bm{w})^{d-1}}{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})^{d-1}}\left[\frac{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})-z_{q}(\bm{w})}{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(\bm{w})-r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}\right]^{-1-1/\xi}\{1+o(1)\}\to(1+\xi z)_{+}^{-1-1/\xi},\qquad\text{as $q\to 1$}.

Consider first the integral term in expression (
A.8
). The integral is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}

\displaystyle s^{d-1}f_{\bm{X}}(s\bm{w})~\mathrm{d}s=\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}s^{d-1}\exp[-\psi_{\textsf{L}}(s)\{-\{\log f_{\bm{X}}(s\bm{w})\}/\psi_{\textsf{L}}(s)\}]\mathrm{d}s.

Due to Lemma
1
with

V(\bm{x})=-\log f_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x})
and
h=\psi
, for all

\varepsilon>0
there exists
q_{0}>q
such that

\displaystyle\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}s^{d-1}\exp[-(1+\varepsilon)\psi_{\textsf{L}}(s)g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}]\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}s^{d-1}f_{\bm{X}}(s\bm{w})~\mathrm{d}s\leq\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}\exp[-(1-\varepsilon)\psi_{\textsf{L}}(s)g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}]\mathrm{d}s.

Since
\varepsilon
can be made arbitrarily small, we have

\displaystyle\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}s^{d-1}f_{\bm{X}}(s\bm{w})~\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}s^{d-1}\exp[-\psi_{\textsf{L}}(s)g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}]\mathrm{d}s

Without loss,
\psi_{\textsf{L}}
can be taken smooth (Lemma 1.4,
Seneta 1976
) so that
d^{k}\psi_{\textsf{L}}(t)/dt^{k}
exists for all
t>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}s^{d-1}f_{\bm{X}}(s\bm{w})~\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}s^{d-1}\left[\frac{\frac{d}{ds}\exp[-\psi_{\textsf{L}}(s)g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}]}{-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}\psi^{\prime}_{\textsf{L}}(s)}\right]\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})^{d-1}\left[\frac{\exp[-\psi_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}]}{g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}\psi^{\prime}_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))}\right]\{1+o(1)\},\qquad\text{as $q\to 1$}.

Using Lemma
1
again, we have that equation (
A.8
) converges to

\displaystyle\frac{r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})z_{q}(\bm{w})^{d-1}}{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})^{d-1}}g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}\psi^{\prime}_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))\exp[-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}\{\psi_{\textsf{L}}(z_{q}(\bm{w}))-\psi_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))\}]\{1+o(1)\}

\displaystyle=r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}\psi^{\prime}_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))\exp[-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}\{\psi_{\textsf{L}}(z_{q}(\bm{w}))-\psi_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))\}]\{1+o(1)\},\qquad\text{as $q\to 1$}.

A Taylor expansion of
\psi_{\textsf{L}}(z_{q}(\bm{w}))
about

r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}
and local uniform convergence give

\psi_{\textsf{L}}(z_{q}(\bm{w}))=\psi_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))+\psi^{\prime}_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})+o(1)
. Thus,

\displaystyle r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}\psi^{\prime}_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))\exp[-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}\{\psi^{\prime}_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})+o(1)\}]\{1+o(1)\}

\displaystyle=r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}\psi^{\prime}_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))\exp[-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}\{\psi^{\prime}_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})]\}]\{1+o(1)\}\to\exp(-z),\qquad\text{as $q\to 1$},

whenever

r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})\sim\{\psi^{\prime}_{\textsf{L}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}))g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{\rho}\}^{-1}
.

Consider first the integral term in expression (
A.8
). The integral is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}f_{R,\bm{W}}(s,\bm{w})~\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}s^{d-1}\{f_{\bm{X}}(s\bm{w})/\psi_{\textsf{H}}(s)\}\{\psi_{\textsf{H}}(s)/s^{1-d}\}~\mathrm{d}s.

Due to convergence
(iii)
, the map

(0,\infty)\ni s\mapsto\{f_{\bm{X}}(s\bm{w})/\psi_{\textsf{H}}(s)\}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is slowly varying at
\infty

for any
\bm{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Further, convergence
(iii)
implies
that
\psi_{\mathsf{H}}(s)\in\text{RV}_{-(d+\xi^{-1})}^{\infty}
,
hence there exists
l_{\textsf{H}}\in\text{RV}_{0}^{\infty}
such that

\psi_{\mathsf{H}}(s)=l_{\mathsf{H}}(s)s^{-\{d+(1/\xi)\}}
. By Karamata’s theorem
(
Resnick 2007
, Theorem
2.1)
,

\frac{\int_{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}^{\infty}f_{R,\bm{W}}(s,\bm{w})~\mathrm{d}s}{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})f_{R,\bm{W}}(r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}),\bm{w})}=\xi\,\{1+o(1)\},\qquad\text{as $q\to 1$}.

Thus, when
r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})=\xi\,r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})
and as
q\to 1
,
expression (
A.8
) converges to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi}

\displaystyle\,\frac{r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})}{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}\left[\frac{z_{q}(\bm{w})}{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}\right]^{d-1}\frac{f_{\bm{X}}[z_{q}(\bm{w})\bm{w}]}{f_{\bm{X}}[r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})\,\bm{w}]}\{1+o(1)\}=\left[1+\frac{r_{\mathcal{G}_{q}}(\bm{w})z}{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}\right]^{-1-1/\xi}\{1+o(1)\}\to(1+\xi z)_{+}^{-1-1/\xi}.

∎

A.6
Stability of the radial Exponential distribution

Proof of stability equation (
44
).

Let the random vector
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
follow an
rExp
distribution
with location
\mathcal{M}
, scale
\Sigma
, and directional component

\mathcal{W}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Z}\in[\mathcal{M}+B_{r_{1}+r_{2}}(\bm{0})\cdot\Sigma]^{\prime}\mid\bm{Z}\in\{\mathcal{M}+B_{r_{1}}(\bm{0})\cdot\Sigma\}^{\prime}\right]=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Z}\in\{\mathcal{M}+B_{r_{1}+r_{2}}(\bm{0})\cdot\Sigma\}^{\prime}\right]}{\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Z}\in\{\mathcal{M}+B_{r_{1}}(\bm{0})\cdot\Sigma\}^{\prime}\right]}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left[\{\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert-r_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\bm{Z}/\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert\right)\}/r_{\Sigma}\left(\bm{Z}/\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert\right)>r_{1}+r_{2}\right]}{\mathbb{P}\left[\{\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert-r_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\bm{Z}/\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert\right)\}/r_{\Sigma}\left(\bm{Z}/\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert\right)>r_{1}\right]}=\exp\{-r_{2}\}=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Z}\in\{\mathcal{M}+B_{r_{2}}(\bm{0})\}^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma\right].

∎

A.7
Stability of the radial generalised Pareto distribution

Proof of stability equation (
41
).

Let the random vector
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
follow an
rGP
distribution
with location
\mathcal{M}
, scale
\Sigma
, shape
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
directional component
\mathcal{W}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Z}\in[\mathcal{M}+B_{r_{1}+r_{2}}(\bm{0})\cdot\Sigma]^{\prime}\mid\bm{Z}\in\{\mathcal{M}+B_{r_{1}}(\bm{0})\cdot\Sigma\}^{\prime}\right]=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Z}\in\{\mathcal{M}+B_{r_{1}+r_{2}}(\bm{0})\cdot\Sigma\}^{\prime}\right]}{\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Z}\in\{\mathcal{M}+B_{r_{1}}(\bm{0})\cdot\Sigma\}^{\prime}\right]}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left[\{\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert-r_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\bm{Z}/\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert\right)\}/r_{\Sigma}\left(\bm{Z}/\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert\right)>r_{1}+r_{2}\right]}{\mathbb{P}\left[\{\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert-r_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\bm{Z}/\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert\right)\}/r_{\Sigma}\left(\bm{Z}/\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert\right)>r_{1}\right]}=\frac{\left[1+\xi(r_{1}+r_{2})\right]_{+}^{-1/\xi}}{\left[1+\xi r_{1}\right]_{+}^{-1/\xi}}=\left[1+\xi\frac{r_{2}}{1+\xi r_{1}}\right]^{-1/\xi}

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Z}\in\left\{\mathcal{M}+\frac{B_{r_{2}}(\bm{0})\cdot\Sigma}{B_{1}(\bm{0})+B_{\xi}(\bm{0})\cdot B_{r_{1}}(\bm{0})}\right\}^{\prime}\right].

∎

A.8
Marginal density of directional variable under homothetic density

Proof of Proposition (
7
).

Suppose that
f(\bm{x})=f_{0}(g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x}))
,
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then,

\displaystyle f_{\bm{W}}(\bm{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{R,\bm{W}}(r,\bm{w})\,\mathrm{d}r=\frac{1}{g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{d}}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{d-1}f_{0}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\frac{1}{d\,\lvert\mathcal{G}\rvert\,g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{d}},

(A.9)

where the last equality follows from
Balkema & Nolde 2010
.
∎

Supplementary Material for “Statistical inference for radially-stable generalized Pareto distributions and return level-sets in geometric extremes”

Appendix 2
Technical background

2.1
Vague convergence

For a detailed exposition of the concept of vague convergence, see

Resnick 2007
. Let
\mathcal{E}
be a locally compact metric space
with countable base (e.g.,
\mathbb{R}^{d}
) and let
M_{+}(\mathcal{E})
be the class of
nonnegative Radon measures on Borel subsets of
\mathcal{E}
. If

\mu_{n}\in M_{+}(\mathcal{E})
for
n\geq 0
, then we say that
\mu_{n}
converges
vaguely to
\mu
(presented as
\mu_{n}\,\overset{\mathrm{v}}{\rightarrow}\,\mu
) if

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{E}}fd\mu_{n}=\int_{\mathcal{E}}fd\mu.

for all bounded continuous functions
f
with compact support.

2.2
Multivariate regular variation

To understand radial stability properties in a
d
-dimensional setup,
we revisit classical multivariate extreme value theory through
consideration of multivariate regular variation (MRV). Given a
random vector
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a common assumption is that
its margins follow the same univariate distribution. If this is not
guaranteed, standardisation is required through the probability
integral transform with the true distribution function and the
desired target quantile function. When the true distribution
function is not known, the empirical distribution function is used
in the bulk of the data, while the Generalised Pareto distribution
function with fitted parameters is used outside of high left and
right thresholds to avoid issues the arise when using the empirical
distribution function in the tail
(
Coles & Tawn 1991
)
.
Under the choice of standard exponential margins,
a random vector
\bm{X}
is multivariate regularly varying on the cone

\mathcal{E}=[-\infty,\infty]^{d}\setminus\{-\infty\}^{d}
if there
exists a limit measure
\nu_{\textsf{MRV}}
such that as
n\to\infty

n\,\mathbb{P}[\bm{X}-(\log n)\bm{1}\in\cdot]\,\overset{\mathrm{v}}{\rightarrow}\,\nu_{\textsf{MRV}}(\cdot),\quad\text{in $M_{+}(\mathcal{E})$},

(A.10)

A consequence of convergence (
A.10
) is that

\nu_{\textsf{MRV}}(t\bm{1}+B)=\exp(-t)\nu_{\textsf{MRV}}(B)
,
implying domain of attraction properties for the distribution of

\bm{X}
. For instance, after appropriate recentering,
the distribution function of the componentwise maxima

\bm{M}_{n}:=(\max_{i=1,\dots,n}X_{ij}\,:\,j=1,\dots,d)
of a
random sample
\bm{X}_{i}=(X_{ij}\,:\,j=1,\dots,d)
,
i=1,\dots,n
, from
the distribution of
\bm{X}
, converges weakly to

\mathbb{P}\left(\bm{M}_{n}-(\log n)\bm{1}\leq\bm{z}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left\{\bm{X}_{i}-(\log n)\bm{1}\in[-\bm{\infty},\bm{z}]\,:\,i=1,\dots,n\right\}\,\overset{\mathrm{w}}{\rightarrow}\,\exp\left[-\Lambda_{\textsf{MRV}}(\bm{z})\right],

where the exponent function

\Lambda_{\textsf{MRV}}(\bm{z}):=\nu_{\textsf{MRV}}([-\infty,\bm{z}]^{\prime})
. The measure
\Lambda_{\textsf{MRV}}
is in one-to-one
correspondence with a Radon measure
H_{\textsf{MRV}}
on the
unit-simplex

\Delta_{+}^{d-1}=\{\bm{\omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\,:\,\lVert\bm{w}\rVert_{1}=1\}
, where

\lVert\bm{x}\rVert_{p}=(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lvert x_{i}\rvert^{p})^{1/p}
for

\bm{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, termed the spectral measure,
satisfying the mass–moment constraint
H_{\textsf{MRV}}(\Delta_{+}^{d-1})=d
and

\int_{\Delta_{+}^{d-1}}w_{i}H(d\bm{w})=1
, for
i=1,\dots,d
. The
correspondence is given by

\Lambda_{\textsf{MRV}}(B)=\int_{\Delta_{+}^{d-1}}\max\left[\bm{w}\exp(-\bm{x})\right]H_{\textsf{MRV}}(d\bm{w}),

where vector algebra is interpreted component-wise. Similarly, the
vector of renormalised threshold exceedances converges in distribution
to a multivariate Pareto distribution. That is,

\lim_{r\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}-r\bm{1}\in[-\bm{\infty},\bm{x}]\mid\max\bm{X}>r\right]=\frac{\Lambda_{\textsf{MRV}}(\bm{x}\wedge\bm{0})-\Lambda_{\textsf{MRV}}(\bm{x})}{\Lambda_{\textsf{MRV}}(\bm{0})},

at continuity points
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{E}
of the limit distribution
function of the multivariate generalised Pareto distribution, where

\Lambda_{\textsf{MRV}}(\bm{x})=\nu_{\textsf{MRV}}([-\infty,\bm{x}]^{\prime})
. Multivariate generalised Pareto distributions arise as the
only non-trivial limit distributions when renormalised multivariate
exceedances are considered
(
Rootzén & Tajvidi 2006
)
. These distributions
possess appealing theoretical and practical properties

Engelke & Hitz 2020
and are often thought to be the
multivariate analogue of the generalised Pareto distribution

Pickands 1975
by being the only threshold-stable multivariate
distributions
(
Kiriliouk et al. 2019
)
. The practical importance of
their threshold-stability lies in the preservation of the model’s
distributional type at higher levels, which is advantageous for
extrapolation into the joint tail region of the
distribution.

Both component-wise maxima and multivariate threshold exceedance
approaches can be unified under a single theme. Let
r_{q}
be the

q
-th quantile of the distribution of
\lVert\bm{X}\rVert_{1}
. Then
the overarching assumption that unifies these approaches is that as

q\to 1
, the point process of recentered exceedances

\bm{X}_{i}-r_{q}\bm{1}\mid\lVert\bm{X}_{i}\rVert_{1}>r_{q}
, converges in
distribution to a Poisson point process on

(0,\infty\,]\times\Delta_{+}^{d-1}
with mean measure

\displaystyle\lim_{q\to 1}\mathbb{P}\left[\lVert\bm{X}\rVert_{1}-r_{q}>z,\frac{\bm{X}}{\lVert\bm{X}\rVert_{1}}\in B~\Big|~\lVert\bm{X}\rVert_{1}>r_{q}\right]=\exp(-z)H_{\textsf{MRV}}(B),\quad\text{$z>0$,
$B\subset\Delta_{+}^{d-1}$.}

(A.11)

In practice, convergence (
A.11
) is typically assumed to
hold with
H_{\textsf{MRV}}
placing mass in the interior of the unit-simplex

\Delta_{+}^{d-1}
,

(\Delta_{+}^{d-1})^{\circ}:=\{\bm{\omega}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\,:\,\min_{i}\omega_{i}>0,\lVert\bm{\omega}\rVert_{1}=1\}
. Although useful for theoretical
investigations,
H_{\textsf{MRV}}
placing mass in
(\Delta_{+}^{d-1})^{\circ}
as a

dependence assumption
can be inadequate in practice since it
imposes a strong form of dependence in the joint tail, which may not
be supported by the data at hand, see for example
Section
4
.

2.3
Background on starshaped sets

An expository review and survey of material about the fundamental
mathematical notion of starshaped sets, emphasising their
geometric, analytical, combinatorial, and topological properties, is
given by
Hansen et al. 2020
and references therein.

If
\bm{a}
and
\bm{b}
are different elements of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then

[\bm{a}:\bm{b}]=\{(1-t)\bm{a}+t\bm{b}\,:\,0\leq t\leq 1\}
denotes the segment with
endpoints
\bm{a}
and
\bm{b}
,
[\bm{a}:\bm{b})=\{(1-t)\bm{a}+t\bm{b}\,:\,t\geq 0\}
denotes
the half-line with origin
\bm{a}
through
\bm{b}
,

(\bm{a}:\bm{b})=\{(1-t)\bm{a}+t\bm{b}\,:\,0\leq t\in\mathbb{R}\}
denotes the line through

\bm{a}
and
\bm{b}
,
]\bm{a}:\bm{b}]=\{(1-t)\bm{a}+t\bm{b}\,:\,0<t\leq 1\}
denotes the
segment
[\bm{a}:\bm{b}]
without its endpoint
\bm{a}
,

[\bm{a}:\bm{b}[=\{(1-t)\bm{a}+t\bm{b}\,:\,0\leq t<1\}
denotes the segment

[\bm{a}:\bm{b}]
without its endpoint
\bm{b}
and

]\bm{a}:\bm{b})=\{(1-t)\bm{a}+t\bm{b}\,:\,t>0\}
denotes the ray
[\bm{a},\bm{b})
without
its origin
\bm{a}
. A set
G\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called starshaped if there
exists
\bm{x}\in G
such that every point
\bm{y}\in G
is visible from
\bm{x}
in
the sense that
[\bm{x}:\bm{y}]\subset G
. If
G
is a set and
\bm{x}\in G
, the
star of
\bm{x}
in
G
is the set of all points in
G
that are visible
from
\bm{x}
, that is,
\text{st}(\bm{x}:G)=\{\bm{y}\in G\,:\,[\bm{x}:\bm{y}]\subset G\}
. A
set
G
is starshaped if and only if there exists
\bm{x}\in G
such that

\text{st}(\bm{x}:G)=G
. The kernel of a set
G
, denoted by

\text{ker}\,G
, is the convex set of all of its star centres, that
is,
\text{ker}\,G=\{\bm{x}\in G\,:\,\text{st}(\bm{x}:G)=G\}
. A star set is
called a star-body if it is a compact set on a regular domain, that is if
G

is a compact starshaped set such that
G^{\circ}
is connected and

G=\overline{G^{\circ}}
. Let
G
be a closed proper subset of

\mathbb{R}^{d}
with non-empty interior. Then
G
is said to be strongly
starshaped at
\bm{a}
whenever there exists
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for
every
\bm{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, the halfline
\bm{a}+[\bm{0}:\bm{w})
does not
intersect the boundary
\partial G
of
G
more than once. A strongly
starshaped set at
a
is a starshaped set with

a\in\text{ker}\,G
. A set
G
is said to be strongly starshaped
if it is strongly starshaped at
\bm{0}
. We write
\Delta_{\bm{w}}
for
the half-line through the origin
[\bm{0}:\bm{w})
passing through

\bm{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. If
G
is starshaped at
\bm{m}\in\text{ker}\,G
,
we say that
\bm{z}\in G
is the last point of a ray
[\bm{m}:\bm{x})
in
G
if

\bm{z}\in[\bm{m}:\bm{x})
and there is no point
\bm{y}\in[\bm{m}:\bm{x})\cap G
such that

\bm{y}\notin[\bm{m}:\bm{z}]
. Let
G
be a starshaped set such that

(\text{ker}\,G)^{\circ}\not=\varnothing
. If

\bm{m}\in(\text{ker}\,G)^{\circ}
and
\bm{x}\in\overline{G}
, then

[\bm{m}:\bm{x}[\subset G
. If
G
is a closed starshaped set such that

(\text{ker}\,G)^{\circ}\not=\varnothing
, then
G^{\circ}
is connected
and
G=\overline{(G^{\circ})}
, whence a regular domain.

2.4
Radial addition and gauges of starshaped sets

If
G
is a closed starshaped set,
\bm{m}\in\text{ker}\,G
and

\bm{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, then there are two cases. There exists a last
point
\bm{p}
of
\bm{m}+\Delta_{\bm{w}}
in
G
or

\bm{m}+\Delta_{\bm{w}}\subset G
. In the first case, let

\rho_{\bm{m},G}(\bm{w})=\sup\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,\bm{m}+\lambda\bm{w}\in G\}.

The
radial function
of
G
at
\bm{m}
is the function

r_{\bm{m},G}\,:\,\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined by

r_{\bm{m},G}(\bm{w})=\begin{cases}\rho_{\bm{m},G}(\bm{w})&\bm{m}+\Delta_{\bm{w}}\not\subseteq G\\
+\infty&\text{$\bm{m}+\Delta_{\bm{w}}\subseteq G$.}\end{cases}

We have the equivalence

\bm{m}\in(\text{ker}\,G)^{\circ}\Leftrightarrow r_{\bm{m},G}>0
. Also, if

G
is compact and
\bm{m}\in(\text{ker}\,G)^{\circ}
, then
r_{\bm{m},G}
is a
Lipschitz function.

Let
\bigstar
denote the space of star-bodies in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

+,\cdot\,:\,\bigstar\times\bigstar\to\bigstar
be the
operators of
radial addition
and
radial
multiplication
respectively, defined by means of radial functions
as as

\displaystyle\rho_{G_{1}+G_{2}}(\bm{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{G_{1}}(\bm{w})+\rho_{G_{2}}(\bm{w}),\qquad G_{1},G_{2}\in\bigstar,

(A.12)

\displaystyle\rho_{G_{1}\cdot G_{2}}(\bm{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{G_{1}}(\bm{w})\rho_{G_{2}}(\bm{w}),\qquad G_{1},G_{2}\in\bigstar,

(A.14)

as illustrated in Figure
8
. Multiplication of a set
G\in\bigstar
by a nonnegative scalar
c

is equivalent to radial multiplication of
G
by
B_{c}(\bm{0})
, that is,

cG:=B_{c}(\bm{0})\cdot G
,
c\geq 0
. The set
\bigstar
equiped with the binary
operation
+
is a commutative monoid. In other words,

(\bigstar,+)
satisfies the following.

Identity element:

There exists an element
\bm{0}\in\bigstar
such
that
\bm{0}+G=G+\bm{0}=G

\forall G\in\bigstar
.

Commutative law:

For
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
in
\bigstar
,

G_{1}+G_{2}=G_{2}+G_{1}
.

Additionally, we have

Scalar Multiplication Closure:

The result of
\cdot

between a compact star set and a non-negative scalar remains within
the space of compact star sets.

Distributive Laws:

The operations
+
and
\cdot
obey
distributive laws similar to those in a ring. For example,

(G_{1}+G_{2})\cdot B_{c}(\bm{0})=G_{1}\cdot B_{c}(\bm{0})+G_{2}\cdot B_{c}(\bm{0})
.

Associativity:

Both
+
and

\cdot
are associative operations. For example,
(G_{1}+G_{2})+G_{3}=G_{1}+(G_{2}+G_{3})
.

The monoid
(\bigstar,+)
is endowed with its algebraic
preordering
\leq
defined by
G_{1}\leq G_{2}
whenever there exists

H\in\bigstar
such that
G_{1}+H=G_{2}
. In this case, we define
radial subtraction
H=G_{2}-G_{1}
through ordinary difference of
radial functions.

The algebraic structure on the space of compact star sets with the
operations
+
and
\cdot
provides a mathematical
framework to explore relationships, transformations, and compositions
of these sets. This algebraic approach facilitates the development of
consistent theories and methodologies when dealing with operations
involving compact star sets.

Last, let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be two star-bodies such that
\rho_{S_{i}}
is
continuous on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Then the dual Brunn-Minkowski inequality

(
Lutwak 1988
)
yields

\lvert G_{1}+G_{2}\rvert^{1/d}\leq\lvert G_{1}\rvert^{1/d}+\lvert G_{2}\rvert^{1/d},

and equality holds if
d=1
or
d\geq 2
and
G_{2}=rG_{1}
, for some

r>0
. If
G
is compact and strongly starshaped, then the volume of

G
can be expressed by the formula

\lvert G\rvert=\frac{1}{d}\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{d}d\bm{w},

(A.15)

see
Klain 1997
. The reciprocal of the radial function
of a set
G
starshaped at
\bm{0}
is called the gauge function

g_{G}\,:\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, defined by

g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})=\inf\{t\geq 0\,:\,\bm{x}\in tG\}
for
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
.

Figure 8
:
Left:
\mathcal{M}+\mathcal{G}
. Centre:
\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{G}
. Right:

\mathcal{M}+B_{-\log(1-q)}(\bm{0})\cdot\mathcal{G}
, for
B_{r}(\bm{x})
the ball of radius
r
centred at
\bm{x}
, and
q\in\{0.9,0.99,0.999\}
.
\mathcal{M}
in lightest grey,
\mathcal{G}
in mid grey, result of operations in
dark grey.

Appendix 3
Remainder term and quality of convergence

3.1
Rates of convergence

Let

f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})=\exp\{-g_{\mathcal{G}_{L}}(t\bm{x})+\text{remainder}\}(1+o(1)),\quad\text{as $t\to\infty$},

(A.16)

where the remainder is interpreted as the evaluation
u(t\bm{x})

of a function
u\,:\,\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{R}
at

t\bm{x}\in\mathbb{K}
. The domain of the function
u
is assumed
to be a solid cone
\mathbb{K}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
. A characterisation of
the map
u
under second-order conditions
(
de Haan & Stadtmüller 1996
)

is given in Section
3.1
. A functional

u\,:\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\to\mathbb{R}
is said to determine the rate of convergence
to
g_{\mathcal{G}}
in expression (
33
) if it satisfies

f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})=\exp\left(-\left[tg_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})\{1+o(1)\}\right]\right)=\exp\left[-\left\{g_{\mathcal{G}}(t\bm{x})+u(t\bm{x})\right\}\right]\{1+o(1)\},\quad\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\},

(A.17)

as
t\to\infty
. Two functionals
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
that
determine the rate of convergence to
g_{\mathcal{G}}
are asymptotically
equivalent, that is,

\lim_{t\to\infty}u_{1}(t\bm{x})/u_{2}(t\bm{x})=1
,
\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
. As such, the set of all functionals

u
satisfying expression (
A.17
) forms an equivalence
class. To better understand the properties of the members of this
class, it is helpful to consider the special case where the leading
order term of

u
is equal to the product of a positive scaling function

l_{1}(t)
and a function
u_{1}(\bm{x})
, that is, when
u(t\bm{x})=O(l_{1}(t)u_{1}(\bm{x}))
as
t\to\infty
, for
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
. Assumption
2
delineates a sufficient
condition subject to which the rate of convergence has such a
behaviour.

Assumption 2
.

Suppose that condition
(ii)
of Proposition
6
holds. There exists a non-decreasing function

l_{1}\,:\,(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
and a function

u_{1}\,:\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\to\mathbb{R}
which is not everywhere zero, such that

\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\{-\log f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})/t\}-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})}{a_{1}(t)}=u_{1}(\bm{x}),

(A.18)

where
a_{1}(t)=l_{1}(t)/t
for
t>0
.

Due to condition
(ii)
of Proposition
6
we have that the
scale sequence
a_{1}(t)=o(1)
as
t\to\infty
. This implies
that
l_{1}(t)=o(t)
as
t\to\infty
. Also
Assumption (
2
) requires that
-\log f_{[L]}
is a

multivariate extended regularly varying
function in the sense
of Definition 1 of
Papastathopoulos & Paulin 2023
. To be precise,
convergence (
A.18
) is equivalent to assuming that there exists

p_{1}\,:\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\to\mathbb{R}
which is not everywhere zero, such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{[-\log f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})/g_{\mathcal{G}}(t\bm{x})]-[-\log f_{[L]}(t\bm{1})/g_{\mathcal{G}}(t\bm{1})]}{a_{1}(t)}=p_{1}(\bm{x}),\qquad\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}.

(A.19)

The correspondence between the two limit functions in (
A.18
)
and (
A.19
) is

p_{1}(\bm{x})=[u_{1}(\bm{x})/g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})]-[u_{1}(\bm{1})/g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{1})]
. From this, it immediately follows that
p_{1}(\bm{1})=0
. Also, it is known that the only possible non-trivial limit functions
that can arise from convergence (
A.19
) are those that satisfy
the functional equation

[p_{1}(t\bm{x})-p_{1}(t\bm{1})]/t^{\gamma-1}=p_{1}(\bm{x}),\qquad t>0,\qquad\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\},

for some
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, see Theorem 2 of
Papastathopoulos & Paulin 2023
for a
proof. These observations lead to Proposition
10
which
identifies the key properties of the scaling function

a_{1}(t)=l_{1}(t)/t
and of the limit function
u_{1}(\bm{x})

under Assumption (
2
).

Proposition 10
.

Suppose that Assumption
2
holds. There exists

\gamma<1
such that
a_{1}(t)\in\textsf{RV}_{\gamma-1}^{\infty}

and
u_{1}
is
\gamma
–homogeneous, that is,

u_{1}(t\bm{x})=t^{\gamma}u_{1}(\bm{x})

\forall t>0
and

\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
.

From Proposition
10
we obtain

l_{1}(t)\in\text{RV}_{\gamma}^{\infty}
. Because Assumption
2
requires
l_{1}(t)
to be a non-decreasing function,
it suffices to consider the following two cases. First, the case when

l_{1}(t)
converges to a constant
K\in(0,\infty)
and second, the
case when
l_{1}(t)
grows without bound. For the former case we
consider without loss of generality the case
l_{1}(t)=1

\forall t>0
. This is because positive real constants can be
absorbed in the limit function
u_{1}
. Below, we treat the two
cases in turn.

First, when
l_{1}(t)=1
, the leading order term of
u(t\bm{x})

is constant in
t
which implies that the rate of convergence is
determined by the function
u_{1}
, that is,

u(t\bm{x})=u_{1}(t\bm{x})+o(1)
, as
t\to\infty
. Also,
because
l_{1}\in\textsf{RV}_{0}^{\infty}
, Proposition
10

yields that
u_{1}
is a
0
–homogeneous function. Consequently,

f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})=\exp\left[-\left\{tg_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})+u_{1}(\bm{x})\right\}\right]\{1+o(1)\},\qquad\text{as $t\to\infty$}.

(A.20)

This special case gives a characterisation of the rate of convergence
which is independent of the distance from the origin
t
. This
behaviour is specific to the case
l(t)=1
and is waived when

l_{1}(t)
grows without bound because in the latter case, the rate
of convergence may have non-negligible remainder terms.

Using the same working principle as in Assumption
2
, below
in Assumption
3
we outline a sufficient condition subject
to which the rate of convergence exhibits a so-called

second-order
behaviour
(
de Haan & Stadtmüller 1996
,
Papastathopoulos & Paulin 2023
)
.

Assumption 3
.

Suppose that Assumption
2
holds with

\lim_{t\to\infty}l_{1}(t)=\infty
. There exists a
non-decreasing function
l_{2}
and a function

u_{2}\,:\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\to\mathbb{R}
which is not equal to the product of

u_{1}
and a
0
–homogeneous function on

\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
, such that for
a_{2}(t)=l_{2}(t)/l_{1}(t)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{([\{-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})/t\}-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})]/a_{1}(t))-u_{1}(\bm{x})}{a_{2}(t)}=u_{2}(\bm{x}).

(A.21)

It is clear that when Assumption
3
holds with

l_{2}(t)=1
, then

f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})=\exp[-\{tg_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})+l_{1}(t)\,u_{1}(\bm{x})+u_{2}(\bm{x})\}\{1+o(1)],\qquad\text{as $t\to\infty$}.

(A.22)

More generally, we can construct an asymptotic expansion of

-\log f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})
of the form

-\log f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})=g_{\mathcal{G}}(t\bm{x})+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}l_{j}(t)u_{j}(\bm{x}),\quad\text{as $t\to\infty$}.

(A.23)

for a sequence of positive scale functions
\{l_{j}\}_{j=1}^{M}
with

M\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, satisfying
l_{j}(t)=o(l_{j+1}(t))
as

t\to\infty
, and a sequence of functions
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{M}

mapping
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
into
\mathbb{R}
. The function
u_{j}
captures the
contribution of
-\log f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})
at a given order of the series and

l_{j}
is the scaling factor associated with this term. The
j
-th
term
l_{j}(t)u_{j}(\bm{x})
in the sum represents the
j
-th
approximation to
-\log f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})
as
t\to\infty
, and the scaling
factors
l_{j}
determine the rate of convergence to
u_{j-1}
,
with
u_{0}(\bm{x}):=g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})
.

:

g_{\mathcal{G}}
of limit set

\mathcal{G}
.

limit function
u_{1}
.

u_{2}
.

alternative viewing
angle obtained by rotating the images shown in top row by

about
x
axis

:
Limit functions in asymptotic expansion of the logarithm of
the density of the bivariate max-stable copula with logistic
dependence and Laplace marginal distributions.

gauge function
g_{\mathcal{G}}
of limit set
\mathcal{G}
.
Centre:
limit
function
u_{1}
.
Right:
Limit function
u_{2}
.
Bottom:
alternative viewing angle obtained by rotating
the images shown in top row by
60^{\circ}
about
x
axis

Proposition 11
.

Suppose that Assumption
3
holds for the
random vector
\bm{X}_{L}
. There exists
\gamma<1
and
\rho\leq 0

such that

u_{2}(t\bm{x})=t^{\gamma}u_{2}(\bm{x})+ct^{\gamma}\frac{t^{\rho}-1}{\rho}u_{1}(\bm{x}).

and the solution of this functional equation is

u_{2}(\bm{x})=\lVert\bm{x}\rVert^{\gamma+\rho}h\left(\frac{\bm{x}}{\lVert\bm{x}\rVert}\right)+c\lVert\bm{x}\rVert^{\gamma}\log\lVert\bm{x}\rVert u_{1}\left(\frac{\bm{x}}{\lVert\bm{x}\rVert}\right),\qquad\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}.

(A.24)

for a measurable function
h
that maps
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
into
\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition
11
provides key insight into the rate of
convergence to the limit function in Proposition
6

(
ii
). This result is shown to hold in
Appendix
4
for certain known copulas in
Examples
4.1
,
4.2
, and
4.8
. We note that a similar approach can be used
to study the rate of convergence in Proposition
6

(
i
) and (
iii
) for light- and heavy-tailed
distributions.

Proof of Proposition
10
.

The homogeneity of
u_{1}
follows directly from the
correspondence between
u_{1}
and
p_{1}
and the fact that

p_{1}
is
(\gamma-1)
–homogeneous. It remains to show that

a_{1}\in\text{RV}_{\gamma-1}^{\infty}
. For any
a>0
and

\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{[-\log f_{[L]}\{t(a\bm{x})\}/t]-g_{\mathcal{G}}(a\bm{x})}{a_{1}(t)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\frac{[-\log f_{[L]}\{(at)\bm{x}\}/(at)]-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})}{a_{1}(at)}\frac{{a_{1}(at)}}{{a_{1}(t)}}\cdot

By Assumption
2
, the LHS converges to
u_{1}(a\bm{x})

as
t\to\infty
and the second factor of the RHS converges to

u_{1}(\bm{x})
as
t\to\infty
. Hence, the third factor of RHS
must converge as
t\to\infty
which implies that there exists

\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
such that
a_{1}\in\text{RV}_{\gamma-1}^{\infty}
. The case
\gamma>1
is not admissible due to homogeneity of
g_{\mathcal{G}}
as
the latter ensures that the rate of convergence
u(t\bm{x})
is
not growing faster than
g_{\mathcal{G}}(t\bm{x})
, that is,

\lim_{t\to\infty}u(t\bm{x})/g_{\mathcal{G}}(t\bm{x})=0
for all

\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
. When
\gamma=1
we have

a_{1}\in\text{RV}_{-1}^{\infty}
, which implies that

l_{1}(t)\in\text{RV}_{0}^{\infty}
. Thus, the case
\gamma=1
is
not admissible because it requires
l_{1}(t)\to 0
as

t\to\infty
for
u(t\bm{x})/g_{\mathcal{G}}(t\bm{x})
to converge to zero for
all
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
, which contradicts the assumption that

l_{1}
is a non-decreasing function.
∎

Proof of Proposition
11
.

Since for any
a>0
we have

\displaystyle\cfrac{\cfrac{-t^{-1}\log f_{[L]}\{t\,(a\bm{x})\}-g_{\mathcal{G}}(a\bm{x})}{a_{1}(t)}-u_{1}(a\bm{x})}{a_{2}(t)}=\cfrac{a\cfrac{-(at)^{-1}\log f_{[L]}\{(at)\,\bm{x}\}-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})}{a_{1}(at)}\cfrac{a_{1}(at)}{a_{1}(t)}-a^{\gamma}u_{1}(\bm{x})}{a_{2}(at)}\cfrac{a_{2}(at)}{a_{2}(t)}

\displaystyle=\cfrac{a_{2}(at)}{a_{2}(t)}a^{\gamma}\cfrac{\cfrac{-(at)^{-1}\log f_{[L]}\{(at)\,\bm{x}\}-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})}{a_{1}(at)}-au_{1}(\bm{x})}{a_{2}(at)}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+a\cfrac{\Bigg(\cfrac{a_{1}(at)}{a_{1}(t)}-a^{\gamma-1}\Bigg)}{a_{2}(t)}\cfrac{-(at)^{-1}\log f_{[L]}\{(at)\,\bm{x}\}-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})}{a_{1}(at)}\cdot,

it follows that

u_{2}(a\bm{x})=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{a_{2}(at)}{a_{2}(t)}a^{\gamma}u_{2}(\bm{x})+a\lim_{t\to\infty}\cfrac{\Bigg(\cfrac{a_{1}(at)}{a_{1}(t)}-a^{\gamma-1}\Bigg)}{a_{2}(t)}u_{1}(\bm{x}).

Suppose there exist
a>0
, and sequences
t_{n},t_{n}^{\prime}
such that

\lim_{t_{n}\to\infty}\frac{a_{2}(at_{n})}{a_{2}(t_{n})}=A,\lim_{t_{n}^{\prime}\to\infty}\frac{a_{2}(at_{n}^{\prime})}{a_{2}(t_{n}^{\prime})}=A^{\prime},\lim_{t_{n}\to\infty}\cfrac{\Bigg(\cfrac{a_{1}(at_{n})}{a_{1}(t_{n})}-a^{\gamma-1}\Bigg)}{a_{2}(t_{n})}=B\quad\text{and}\lim_{t_{n}^{\prime}\to\infty}\cfrac{\Bigg(\cfrac{a_{1}(at_{n}^{\prime})}{a_{1}(t_{n}^{\prime})}-a^{\gamma-1}\Bigg)}{a_{2}(t_{n}^{\prime})}=B^{\prime},

with
A\neq A^{\prime}
and
B\neq B^{\prime}
. Then, for all

\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
,

0=(A-A^{\prime})a^{\gamma}u_{2}(\bm{x})+a(B-B^{\prime})u_{1}(\bm{x})

By assumption,
u_{2}
is not equal to the product of
u_{1}

and a
0
–homogeneous functional. Hence,
A=A^{\prime}
and
B=B^{\prime}
. This
implies that there exists
\rho\leq 0
and
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{a_{2}(tx)}{a_{2}(t)}=x^{\rho}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{t\to\infty}\cfrac{\Bigg(\cfrac{a_{1}(at)}{a_{1}(t)}-a^{\gamma-1}\Bigg)}{a_{2}(t)}=cx^{\gamma-1}\frac{x^{\rho}-1}{\rho},\qquad\text{$\forall x>0$,}

for a proof see
de Haan & Stadtmüller 1996
. Consequently, we arrive at
the functional equation

u_{2}\left(\lVert\bm{x}\rVert\frac{\bm{x}}{\lVert\bm{x}\rVert}\right)=\lVert\bm{x}\rVert^{\gamma+\rho}u_{2}\left(\frac{\bm{x}}{\lVert\bm{x}\rVert}\right)+c\lVert\bm{x}\rVert^{\gamma}\frac{\lVert\bm{x}\rVert^{\rho}-1}{\rho}u_{1}\left(\frac{\bm{x}}{\lVert\bm{x}\rVert}\right),\qquad\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}.

(A.25)

where
u_{1}
is a
\gamma
–homogeneous functional on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}

and
u_{2}
is an unknown function. Let

u_{2}^{\star}\left(\bm{x}\right)=\lVert\bm{x}\rVert^{\gamma+\rho}\widetilde{\lambda}_{2}\left(\frac{\bm{x}}{\lVert\bm{x}\rVert}\right)+c\lVert\bm{x}\rVert^{\gamma}\frac{\lVert\bm{x}\rVert^{\rho}-1}{\rho}u_{1}\left(\frac{\bm{x}}{\lVert\bm{x}\rVert}\right),\qquad\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\},

(A.26)

where
\widetilde{\lambda}_{2}
be an arbitrary map from

\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
into
\mathbb{R}
. A simple calculation shows that
the function
u_{2}^{\star}
satisfies the functional
equation (
A.25
). This proves that the general solution
of the functional equation (
A.25
) is
u_{2}^{\star}
. Last, by Assumption
3
we have

a_{2}(t)=o(a_{1}(t))
as

t\to\infty
.

∎

Appendix 4
Convergence to gauge functions for
d
-dimensional copulas

4.1
Multivariate normal copula

The negative logarithm of the probability density function of the
standard
d
-dimensional normal copula with standard Laplace margins
and positive-definite precision matrix
\mathsf{Q}
is

standard
d
-dimensional normal copula with standard Laplace margins
and positive-definite precision matrix
\mathsf{Q}
is

\displaystyle-\log f(t\bm{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\log|\mathsf{Q}|+d\log 2+t\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\left|x_{t,i}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x})^{\top}(\mathsf{Q}-I)\bm{H}(t,\bm{x})

(A.27)

where

\bm{H}\,:\,\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
defined by
\bm{H}(t,\bm{x})=(H(t,x_{i})\,:\,i=1,\dots,d)

, for
t>0
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
. By Mill’s ratio, each individual component of
\bm{H}(t,\bm{x})
admits the asymptotic
expansion

\displaystyle H(t,x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}0&\text{$x_{i}=0$}\\
\text{sgn}(x_{i})\left[2(\log 2+\lvert tx_{i}\rvert)-\log\{4\pi(\log 2+\lvert tx_{i}\rvert)\}\right]^{1/2}+o(t^{-1/2})&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(A.28)

as
t\to\infty
, also implying that

\displaystyle H(t,x_{i})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}0&\text{$x_{i}=0$}\\
2(\log 2+\lvert tx_{i}\rvert)-\log\{4\pi(\log 2+\lvert tx_{i}\rvert)\}+o(1)&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(A.29)

as
t\to\infty
.

Suppose that
x_{i}\neq 0
for all

. Since

2(\log 2+|tx_{i}|)>\log\{4\pi(\log 2+|tx_{i}|)\}

for all sufficiently large

where

\displaystyle a_{k}(t,x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{1/2\choose k}(-1)^{k}[\log\{4\pi(\log 2+t\lvert x_{i}\rvert)\}]^{k}}{\{2(\log 2+t\lvert x_{i}\rvert)\}^{k-(1/2)}}

Let
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{Q}-\mathsf{I}
and consider the quadratic form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x})^{\top}\mathsf{A}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x})=

\displaystyle t\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x})|\bm{x}\rvert^{1/2}\right\}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\,\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x})|\bm{x}\rvert^{1/2}\right\}-t\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}|x_{i}|+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x})|\bm{x}\rvert^{1/2}\right\}^{\top}\mathsf{A}\,\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x})\frac{\log 2}{\lvert\bm{x}\rvert^{1/2}}\right\}+O(t^{-1/2}).

and a similar calculation shows that the terms associated with

Thus,

\displaystyle g(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\text{sgn}(\bm{x})|\bm{x}\rvert^{1/2}\right]^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\,\left[\text{sgn}(\bm{x})|\bm{x}\rvert^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle u_{1}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[\text{sgn}(\bm{x})\lvert\bm{x}\rvert^{1/2}\right]^{\top}\,(\mathsf{Q}-\mathsf{I})\,\left[\text{sgn}(\bm{x})\lvert\bm{x}\rvert^{-1/2}\right]

\displaystyle u_{2}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\text{sgn}(\bm{x})|\bm{x}\rvert^{1/2}\right]^{\top}(\mathsf{Q}-\mathsf{I})\,\left[\text{sgn}(\bm{x})\log 2\lvert\bm{x}\rvert^{-1/2}\right]-

(A.35)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[\text{sgn}(\bm{x})\lvert\bm{x}\rvert^{1/2}\right]^{\top}\,(\mathsf{Q}-\mathsf{I})\,\left[\text{sgn}(\bm{x})\log(4\pi\lvert\bm{x}\rvert)\lvert\bm{x}\rvert^{-1/2}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\log|\mathsf{Q}|+d\log 2

The asymptotic expansion in (
A.33
) is
valid for large
t
when all
x_{i}\neq 0
, and loses validity when at
least one
x_{i}
is equal to 0 This is a result of the asymptotic
expansion of (
A.28
) and (
A.29
), both
depending on
t
and
x_{i}
, resulting in nonuniform behavior. When

x_{i}=0
, the expansions (
A.28
) and (
A.29
)
collapse to zero, while for
x_{i}\neq 0
, the leading term grows as

t^{1/2}
with logarithmic corrections. This difference in growth
rates reflects the nonuniformity in
x_{i}
, as the expansion behaves
differently near the origin and away from it, complicating the
asymptotic analysis of
-\log f(t\bm{x})
whenever
\bm{x}
is near
an axis.

In what follows, we show that a uniform asymptotic expansion exists,
by proving
{-\log f(t\bm{x})}/{t}
converges locally uniformly to a
continuous gauge function via the method of continuous convergence

(
Resnick 2007
)
. That is, we prove that

-\log f(t\bm{x})/t\to g(\bm{x})
as
t\to\infty
, uniformly on compact
sets in the variable
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, by showing that

{-\log f(t\bm{x}_{t})}/{t}
converges to
g(\bm{x})
whenever

\bm{x}_{t}\to\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Consequently, because
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

is compact, the convergence is uniform on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and therefore,
the conditions of Proposition
6
are
satisfied. To prove this assertion, it suffices to consider three disjoint
index sets

A,B,C\subseteq\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}\cup\left\{\emptyset\right\}
such that

A\cup B\cup C=\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
, and

(i)

x_{t,i}\rightarrow x_{i}
and

\left|tx_{t,i}\right|\rightarrow\infty
, where
x_{i}\neq 0
, as

t\rightarrow\infty
for
i\in A
.

(ii)

x_{t,i}\rightarrow 0
and

\left|tx_{t,i}\right|\rightarrow\infty
, as
t\rightarrow\infty

for
i\in B
.

(iii)

x_{t,i}\rightarrow 0
and

\left|tx_{t,i}\right|\rightarrow c_{i}
, where
c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,
as
t\rightarrow\infty
for
i\in C
.

Decomposing the quadratic form in expression
(
A.27
) based on this partition, leads to

\displaystyle\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{t})^{\top}\mathsf{A}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{t})

\displaystyle=\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{A,t})^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{AA}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{A,t})+\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{B,t})^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{BB}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{B,t})+\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{CC}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76228pt+2\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{A,t})^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{AB}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{B,t})+2\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{A,t})^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{AC}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})+2\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{B,t})^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{BC}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{C,t}),
