\displaystyle+\log 2\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})|\bm{x}_{A,t}\rvert^{-1/2}\right\}^{\top}\textsf{A}_{AB}\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{B,t})|\bm{x}_{B,t}\rvert^{1/2}\right\}

\displaystyle+\log 2\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})|\bm{x}_{A,t}\rvert^{1/2}\right\}^{\top}\textsf{A}_{AB}\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{B,t})|\bm{x}_{B,t}\rvert^{-1/2}\right\}

\displaystyle-\left\{\log\left(4\pi\left|\bm{x}_{A,t}\right|\right)\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})|\bm{x}_{A,t}\rvert^{-1/2}\right\}^{\top}\textsf{A}_{AB}\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{B,t})|\bm{x}_{B,t}\rvert^{1/2}\right\}

\displaystyle-\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})|\bm{x}_{A,t}\rvert^{1/2}\right\}^{\top}\textsf{A}_{AB}\left\{\log\left(4\pi\left|\bm{x}_{B,t}\right|\right)\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{B,t})|\bm{x}_{B,t}\rvert^{-1/2}\right\}+O(t^{{1}/{2}})

We note that Mill’s ratio is suitable here, as
\left|tx_{t,i}\right|\rightarrow\infty
as
t\rightarrow\infty
for
i\in A,B
.
Next, we consider the term
\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{A,t})^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{AC}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})
, only applying Mill’s ratio to components in
A
,

\displaystyle\bm{H}

\displaystyle(t,\bm{x}_{A,t})^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{AC}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}[\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})\bm{a}_{k}(t,\bm{x}_{A,t})]^{\top}\,\mathsf{A}_{AC}H(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})(2t|\bm{x}_{A,t}|)^{1/2}+\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})\log 2(2t|\bm{x}_{A,t}|)^{-1/2}+O(t^{-3/2})\right\}^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{AC}H(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})

\displaystyle+\left\{-\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})\frac{\log t}{(2t|\bm{x}_{A,t}|)^{1/2}}-\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})\frac{\log(4\pi|\bm{x}|)}{(2t|\bm{x}_{A,t}|)^{1/2}}+O(t^{-3/2}\log t)\right\}^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{AC}H(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})(2t|\bm{x}_{A,t}|)^{1/2}\right\}^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{AC}H(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})+\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})\log 2(2t|\bm{x}_{A,t}|)^{-1/2}\right\}^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{AC}H(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})

\displaystyle-t^{-1/2}\log t\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})(2|\bm{x}_{A,t}|)^{-1/2}\right\}^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{AC}H(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})-\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{A,t})\frac{\log(4\pi|\bm{x}|)}{(2t|\bm{x}_{A,t}|)^{1/2}}\right\}^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{AC}H(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})

\displaystyle+o(1)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\bm{H}

\displaystyle(t,\bm{x}_{B,t})^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{BC}\bm{H}(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{B,t})(2t|\bm{x}_{B,t}|)^{1/2}\right\}^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{BC}H(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})+\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{B,t})\log 2(2t|\bm{x}_{B,t}|)^{-1/2}\right\}^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{BC}H(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})

\displaystyle-t^{-1/2}\log t\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{B,t})(2|\bm{x}_{B,t}|)^{-1/2}\right\}^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{BC}H(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})-\left\{\text{sgn}(\bm{x}_{B,t})\frac{\log(4\pi|\bm{x}|)}{(2t|\bm{x}_{B,t}|)^{1/2}}\right\}^{\top}\mathsf{A}_{BC}H(t,\bm{x}_{C,t})

\displaystyle+o(1)

Combining all terms together leads to

-\frac{\log f(t\bm{x}_{t})}{t}\to g(\bm{x})\quad\text{whenever
$\bm{x}_{t}\to\bm{x}$},

where
g
is defined in expression (
A.34
).

4.2
Multivariate Laplace copula

The joint density of the
d
-variate Laplace distribution in standard
Laplace margins with positive definite precision matrix

\mathsf{Q}
is

\displaystyle f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})=\frac{2^{1+\frac{d}{2}}\lvert\mathsf{Q}\rvert^{{1}/{2}}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{{d}/{2}}}t^{v}\left(\frac{1}{2^{2}}\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}\right)^{{v}/{2}}K_{v}\left\{t\left(\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}\right)^{{1}/{2}}\right\}

for
t>0
, where
v={(2-d)}/{2}
and
K_{v}
is the modified Bessel
function of the second kind
(
Kotz et al. 2001
)
. Therefore,
-\log f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle=-\left({1+\frac{d}{2}}\right)\log 2+\frac{d}{2}\log\left(2\pi\right)-\frac{1}{2}\log\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|+v\log(2/t)-\frac{v}{2}\log\left(\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}\right)-\log\left[K_{v}\left\{t\left(\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}\right)^{{1}/{2}}\right\}\right]

Asymptotically, we have
K_{v}(z)\sim\left(\pi/2z\right)^{{1}/{2}}e^{-z}\left(1+O(z^{-1})\right)
as
z\rightarrow\infty

(
Gradshteyn & Ryzhik 2014
)
. Applying the
negative logarithm, obtain

\displaystyle-\log K_{v}(z)\sim-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\log z+z+O(z^{-1}).

Substituting this in the expression for
-\log f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})
, obtain

\displaystyle-\log f_{[L]}(t\bm{x})\sim

\displaystyle-\left(1+\frac{d}{2}\right)\log 2+\frac{d}{2}\log 2\pi-\frac{1}{2}\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|-v\log t+v\log 2-\frac{v}{2}\log\left(\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\log t+\frac{1}{4}\log\left(\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}\right)+t\left(\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}\right)^{{1}/{2}}+O(t^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle tg_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})+\left(\log t\right)u_{1}(\bm{x})+u_{2}(\bm{x})+O(t^{-1}),

for
t\rightarrow\infty
, where the gauge function is

g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})=\left(\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}\right)^{{1}/{2}}
. The
higher order terms are given by
u_{1}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{2}-v
and

u_{2}(\bm{x})=-\left(1+\frac{d}{2}-v\right)\log 2+\frac{d}{2}\log 2\pi-\frac{1}{2}\log\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{v}{2}\right)\log\left(\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}\right)
.

4.3
Multivariate max-stable Logistic distribution with standard Fréchet marginal distributions

For positive entries
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, the joint density in standard
Fréchet margins is

\displaystyle f(\bm{x})=\left(\sum\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}(-1)^{|\pi|}\prod\limits_{s\in\pi}V_{s}(\bm{x})\right)\exp\left\{-V(\bm{x})\right\}

where

\smash{V(\bm{x})=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}^{-{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta}}
is a
-1
-homogeneous exponent
function with dependence parameter
\theta\in(0,1)
, and

V_{s}(\bm{x})=\left(-\theta\right)^{-|s|}\left[\prod_{k=0}^{|s|-1}(\theta-k)\right]\left(\prod_{k\in s}x_{k}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\theta}-1}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}^{-{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta-\left|s\right|}

is the
-(\left|s\right|+1)
-homogeneous
\left|s\right|
-order
partial derivative of
V
with respect to inputs whose indices are in

s
. Using the intuition in Proposition
6

(iii)
and
setting
\psi(t)=t^{-(d+1)}
, we have the following convergence

\displaystyle\frac{f(t\bm{x})}{t^{-(d+1)}}\rightarrow(-1)^{d}V_{\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}}(\bm{x})=g(\bm{x})^{-(d+1)}

as
t\rightarrow\infty
, where
V_{\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}}
is the partial derivative of the exponent function with respect to all
d
components. Note that in the context of Proposition
6

(iii)
, this convergence implied that
\xi=1
. In the logistic setting, the expression of
g
is therefore given by

g(\bm{x})=\left(-\theta\right)^{-d}\left[\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}(\theta-k)\right]\left(\prod_{k=1}^{d}x_{k}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\theta}-1}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}^{-{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta-d}

Higher-order terms are given by

\displaystyle u_{k}(\bm{w})

\displaystyle=\frac{f(t\bm{x})-t^{-(d+1)}g(\bm{x})^{-(d+1^{-1})}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}t^{-\sum_{\ell=i+1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}(d+j)}u_{i}(\bm{x})}{t^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}(d+j)}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\sum\limits_{\pi\in\Pi^{(d+1+k)}}(-1)^{|\pi|}\prod\limits_{s\in\pi}V_{s}(\bm{x})\ ;\ t\rightarrow\infty

for
k=1,\dots,d-1
,
where
\Pi^{(n)}\subset\Pi
is such that for all
\pi\in\Pi^{(n)}
,
\sum_{s\in\pi}|s|=n
for
n\in\left\{d+2,d+3,\dots,2d\right\}
.

4.4
Multivariate inverted max-stable distributions, standard exponential margins

The class of multivariate inverted max-stable distributions is usually represented in exponential margins, and has a joint distribution function

F(\bm{x})=e^{-\ell(\bm{x})}

where
\ell
is the 1-homogeneous
stable tail dependence function
, and is defined by the
-1
-homogeneous exponent function
V
, introduced in Supplementary Material
4.3
, through the relation
\ell(\bm{x})=V({1}/{\bm{x}})
. The joint density in exponential margins is given by

f(\bm{x})=\left(\sum\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}(-1)^{|\pi|}\prod\limits_{s\in\pi}\ell_{s}(\bm{x})\right)\exp\left\{-\ell(\bm{x})\right\}

where
\ell_{s}(\bm{x})=V_{s}({1}/{\bm{x}})(-1)^{|s|}\prod_{j\in s}x_{j}^{-2}
is
(1-|s|)
-homogeneous. Here, we use the limiting behaviour in Proposition
6

(ii)
to see limiting behaviour

\displaystyle-\log f(t\bm{x})=tg(\bm{x})+u(\bm{x})

where the gauge function is given by
g(\bm{x})=\ell(\bm{x})
and higher order term

u(\bm{x})=-\log\left[(-1)^{d}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\ell_{\left\{j\right\}}(\bm{x})+o(1)\right]

as
t\rightarrow\infty
.

4.5
Multivariate max-stable and inverted max-stable copula, logistic dependence

The joint density function in Fréchet margins is

\displaystyle f_{F}(\bm{z})=\left(\sum\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}(-1)^{|\pi|}\prod\limits_{s\in\pi}V_{s}(\bm{z})\right)\exp\left\{-V(\bm{z})\right\}

where
\smash{V(\bm{z})=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}z_{j}^{-{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta}}
is a
-1
-homogeneous exponent function with dependence parameter
\theta\in(0,1)
, and
V_{s}
is the
\left|s\right|
-order partial derivative of
V
with respect to inputs whose indices are in
s
. Let
\Pi
be the set of all partitions of the set of indices
\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
, and let
\pi
be the set of all partitions of an arbitraty element in
\Pi
. To obtain the joint density in Laplace margins, change of variables in implemented.

Suppose
z_{t}(x_{j})=z(tx_{j})
for
t>0
and
j\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
. If
x_{j}<0
(or
z_{t}(x_{j})<\left(\log 2\right)^{-1}
), then we perform the change of variables from Fréchet to Laplace margins

\displaystyle z_{t}(x_{j})=

\displaystyle\left(-\log\left(\frac{1}{2}e^{tx_{j}}\right)\right)^{-1}=\left(-tx_{j}\right)^{-1}\left(1-\log 2\left(-tx_{j}\right)^{-1}+O(t^{-2})\right)

with derivative given by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d(tx_{j})}z_{t}(x_{j})=\left(-tx_{j}\right)^{-2}\left(1-2\log 2\left(-tx_{j}\right)^{-1}+O(t^{-2})\right).

If
x_{j}>0
(or
z_{t}(x_{j})>\left(\log 2\right)^{-1}
), then

\displaystyle z_{t}(x_{j})=

\displaystyle\left(-\log\left(1-\frac{1}{2}e^{-tx_{j}}\right)\right)^{-1}=2e^{tx_{j}}-\frac{1}{2}+O\left(e^{-tx_{j}}\right)

with derivative given by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d(tx_{j})}z_{t}(x_{j})=2e^{tx_{j}}+O\left(e^{-tx_{j}}\right).

Lastly, if
x_{j}=0
, then
z_{t}(x_{j})=(\log 2)^{-1}
. By the inverse function and chain rules,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d(tx_{j})}z_{t}(x_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{L}(tx_{j})}{f_{F}\left(F^{-1}_{F}\left(F_{L}(tx_{j})\right)\right)}=\begin{cases}\frac{e^{-t|x_{j}|}}{e^{tx_{j}}\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{2}e^{tx_{j}}\right)\right)^{2}}&;\;\;x_{j}<0\\
\frac{\frac{1}{2}e^{-t|x_{j}|}}{\left(1-\frac{1}{2}e^{-tx_{j}}\right)\left(\log\left(1-\frac{1}{2}e^{-tx_{j}}\right)\right)^{2}}&;\;\;x_{j}>0\end{cases}\xrightarrow{x_{j}\rightarrow 0}(\log 2)^{-2}

For a vector
\bm{x}=\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{d}\right)^{\top}
, let
A,B,C\subset\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
be the set of indices such that
x_{j}
is positive, negative, and zero for
j\in A,B,C
, respectively such that
|A|+|B|+|C|=d
. By change of variables, the joint density for the max-stable distribution with logistic dependence in Laplace margins is

\displaystyle f_{L}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle\left|\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\frac{d}{d(tx_{j})}z_{t}(x_{j})\right|f_{F}\left(z(tx_{1}),\dots,z(tx_{d})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d+1}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}2^{-\frac{|A|}{\theta}}t^{\left(\frac{1}{\theta}-1\right)|B|+1-\frac{d}{\theta}}(\log 2)^{-2|C|}\left(\prod\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{}^{\frac{1}{\theta}-1}}\right)\left(\sum\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta-d}

\displaystyle\times\exp\left\{-t\left[\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{j\in A}x_{j}+\left(\sum\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta}\left(1+O\left(e^{-\frac{t}{\theta}\min_{j\in A}x_{j}}\right)+O(t^{-1})\right)\right]\right\}(1+o(1))

Applying the negative logarithm, obtain the following expression

\displaystyle-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle-\log\left[(-1)^{d+1}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}2^{-\frac{|A|}{\theta}}(\log 2)^{-2|C|}\right]-\left(\frac{1}{\theta}-1\right)\sum\limits_{k\in B}\log(-x_{k})

\displaystyle-(\theta-d)\log\left(\sum\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)+\left\{-\left(\frac{1}{\theta}-1\right)|B|-1+\frac{d}{\theta}\right\}\log t

\displaystyle+t\left[\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{j\in A}x_{j}+\left\{\sum\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right\}^{\theta}\left\{1+O\left(e^{-\frac{t}{\theta}\min_{j\in A}x_{j}}\right)+O(t^{-1})\right\}\right]+o(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle tg(\bm{x})+(\log t)u_{1}(\bm{x})+u_{2}(\bm{x})

where, when letting
t\rightarrow\infty
, the gauge function is

\displaystyle g(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{j\in A}x_{j}+\left\{\sum\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right\}^{\theta}

the higher order terms are given by

\displaystyle u_{1}(\bm{x})=-\left(\frac{1}{\theta}-1\right)|B|-1+\frac{d}{\theta}

and

\displaystyle u_{2}(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle-\log\left[\frac{(-1)^{d+1}2^{-\frac{|A|}{\theta}}}{(\log 2)^{-2|C|}}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{d-1}\left(1{-}\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}\right]{-}\left(\frac{1}{\theta}{-}1\right)\sum\limits_{k\in B}\log(-x_{k}){-}(\theta-d)\log\left\{\sum\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right\}

There are 2 special cases to consider:

•

special case 1:
Suppose
x_{j}>0\;\forall\;j\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
and let
x_{(d)}=\min_{j=1,\dots,d}x_{j}
. Here, the joint log-density is

\displaystyle-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle-\log\left[2^{-1}(-1)^{d}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}\right]+t\left\{\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}+\left(1-\frac{d}{\theta}\right)x_{(d)}\right\}+

\displaystyle+2^{-1}e^{-tx_{(d)}}\left(1+o(1)\right)+o(1)=tg(\bm{x})+u_{1}(\bm{x})+o(1)

where the gauge function is

g(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}+\left(1-\frac{d}{\theta}\right)\min_{k=1,\dots,d}x_{k}

and the higher order term is

u_{1}(\bm{x})=-\log\left[2^{-1}(-1)^{d+1}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}\right],\qquad\text{as $t\rightarrow\infty$.}

•

special case 2:
Suppose
x_{j}<0\;\forall\;j\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
. Here, we have

\displaystyle-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle tg(\bm{x})+(\log t)u_{1}(\bm{x})+u_{2}(\bm{x})+o(1)

where the gauge function is

g(\bm{x})=\left\{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}\left(-x_{j}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right\}^{\theta}

the higher order terms are given by

u_{1}(\bm{x})=d-1

and

u_{2}(\bm{x})=-\log\left[(-1)^{d+1}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}\left\{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\left(-x_{j}\right)\right\}^{\frac{1}{\theta}-1}\left\{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}\left(-x_{j}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right\}^{\theta-d}\right],\qquad\text{as $t\rightarrow\infty$.}

To study the case of
inverted logistic dependence
, first recall the definition of the joint distribution function in Fréchet margins

F_{F}(\bm{z})=\exp\left\{-V(\bm{z})\right\}

By evaluating at the univariate Frechet quantile function, get the Logistic copula

\displaystyle C_{\textit{log.}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{d})=

\displaystyle F_{F}\left(-\frac{1}{\log u_{1}},\dots,-\frac{1}{\log u_{d}}\right)=\exp\left\{-V\left(-\frac{1}{\log u_{1}},\dots,-\frac{1}{\log u_{d}}\right)\right\}

where
V
is specified to be the exponent function commonly associated with logistic dependence. The survival copula associated with the inverted logistic distribution is therefore given by

\displaystyle\bar{C}_{\textit{inv.log.}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{d})=

\displaystyle C_{\textit{log.}}(1-u_{1},\dots,1-u_{d})=\exp\left\{-V\left(-\frac{1}{\log(1-u_{1})},\dots,-\frac{1}{\log(1-u_{d})}\right)\right\}

So
x_{j}>0
(or
u_{j}>{1}/{2}
) in the logistic case corresponds to
x_{j}<0
(or
u_{j}<{1}/{2}
) in the inverted logistic case. This leads to the decomposition of the log-joint density in Laplace margins
-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=tg(\bm{x})+(\log t)u_{1}(\bm{x})+g^{\ast}_{0}(\bm{x})

where the gauge function is

\displaystyle g(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle\left(\sum\limits_{j\in A}x_{j}^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta}+\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{k\in B}(-x_{k})

the higher-order terms are given by

\displaystyle u_{1}(\bm{x})=-\left(\frac{1}{\theta}-1\right)|A|-1+\frac{d}{\theta}

and

\displaystyle u_{2}(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle-\log\left[(-1)^{d+1}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{d-1}\left(1{-}\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}2^{-\frac{|B|}{\theta}}(\log 2)^{-2|C|}\right]{-}\left(\frac{1}{\theta}{-}1\right)\sum\limits_{j\in A}\log(x_{j})-(\theta-d)\log\left(\sum\limits_{j\in A}x_{j}^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)

In the case where
x_{j}>0

\forall j\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
we have
-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=tg(\bm{x})+(\log t)u_{1}(\bm{x})+u_{2}(\bm{x})+o(1)

where the gauge function is

g(\bm{x})=\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta}

with higher-order terms given by

u_{1}(\bm{x})=d-1

and

u_{2}(\bm{x})=-\log\left[(-1)^{d+1}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}\left(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}\right)^{\frac{1}{\theta}-1}\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta-d}\right]

as
t\rightarrow\infty
. In the case where
x_{j}<0

\forall j\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
we have

\displaystyle-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle tg(\bm{x})+u_{1}(\bm{x})+o(1)

where the gauge function is

g(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}(-x_{j})+\left(1-\frac{d}{\theta}\right)\min_{k=1,\dots,d}\left|x_{k}\right|

and the higher order term is

u_{1}(\bm{x})=-\log\left[2^{-1}(-1)^{d}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}\right],\qquad\text{as $t\rightarrow\infty$}.

The joint density function in Fréchet margins is

\displaystyle f_{F}(\bm{z})=\left(\sum\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}(-1)^{|\pi|}\prod\limits_{s\in\pi}V_{s}(\bm{z})\right)\exp\left\{-V(\bm{z})\right\}

where
\smash{V(\bm{z})=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}z_{j}^{-{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta}}
is a
-1
-homogeneous exponent function with dependence parameter
\theta\in(0,1)
, and
V_{s}
is the
\left|s\right|
-order partial derivative of
V
with respect to inputs whose indices are in
s
. Let
\Pi
be the set of all partitions of the set of indices
\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
, and let
\pi
be the set of all partitions of an arbitraty element in
\Pi
. To obtain the joint density in Laplace margins, change of variables in implemented.

Suppose
z_{t}(x_{j})=z(tx_{j})
for
t>0
and
j\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
. If
x_{j}<0
(or
z_{t}(x_{j})<\left(\log 2\right)^{-1}
), then we perform the change of variables from Fréchet to Laplace margins

\displaystyle z_{t}(x_{j})=

\displaystyle\left(-\log\left(\frac{1}{2}e^{tx_{j}}\right)\right)^{-1}=\left(-tx_{j}\right)^{-1}\left(1-\log 2\left(-tx_{j}\right)^{-1}+O(t^{-2})\right)

with derivative given by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d(tx_{j})}z_{t}(x_{j})=\left(-tx_{j}\right)^{-2}\left(1-2\log 2\left(-tx_{j}\right)^{-1}+O(t^{-2})\right).

If
x_{j}>0
(or
z_{t}(x_{j})>\left(\log 2\right)^{-1}
), then

\displaystyle z_{t}(x_{j})=

\displaystyle\left(-\log\left(1-\frac{1}{2}e^{-tx_{j}}\right)\right)^{-1}=2e^{tx_{j}}-\frac{1}{2}+O\left(e^{-tx_{j}}\right)

with derivative given by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d(tx_{j})}z_{t}(x_{j})=2e^{tx_{j}}+O\left(e^{-tx_{j}}\right).

Lastly, if
x_{j}=0
, then
z_{t}(x_{j})=(\log 2)^{-1}
. By the inverse function and chain rules,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d(tx_{j})}z_{t}(x_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{L}(tx_{j})}{f_{F}\left(F^{-1}_{F}\left(F_{L}(tx_{j})\right)\right)}=\begin{cases}\frac{e^{-t|x_{j}|}}{e^{tx_{j}}\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{2}e^{tx_{j}}\right)\right)^{2}}&;\;\;x_{j}<0\\
\frac{\frac{1}{2}e^{-t|x_{j}|}}{\left(1-\frac{1}{2}e^{-tx_{j}}\right)\left(\log\left(1-\frac{1}{2}e^{-tx_{j}}\right)\right)^{2}}&;\;\;x_{j}>0\end{cases}\xrightarrow{x_{j}\rightarrow 0}(\log 2)^{-2}

For a vector
\bm{x}=\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{d}\right)^{\top}
, let
A,B,C\subset\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
be the set of indices such that
x_{j}
is positive, negative, and zero for
j\in A,B,C
, respectively such that
|A|+|B|+|C|=d
. By change of variables, the joint density for the max-stable distribution with logistic dependence in Laplace margins is

\displaystyle f_{L}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle\left|\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\frac{d}{d(tx_{j})}z_{t}(x_{j})\right|f_{F}\left(z(tx_{1}),\dots,z(tx_{d})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d+1}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}2^{-\frac{|A|}{\theta}}t^{\left(\frac{1}{\theta}-1\right)|B|+1-\frac{d}{\theta}}(\log 2)^{-2|C|}\left(\prod\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{}^{\frac{1}{\theta}-1}}\right)\left(\sum\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta-d}

\displaystyle\times\exp\left\{-t\left[\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{j\in A}x_{j}+\left(\sum\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta}\left(1+O\left(e^{-\frac{t}{\theta}\min_{j\in A}x_{j}}\right)+O(t^{-1})\right)\right]\right\}(1+o(1))

Applying the negative logarithm, obtain the following expression

\displaystyle-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle-\log\left[(-1)^{d+1}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}2^{-\frac{|A|}{\theta}}(\log 2)^{-2|C|}\right]-\left(\frac{1}{\theta}-1\right)\sum\limits_{k\in B}\log(-x_{k})

\displaystyle-(\theta-d)\log\left(\sum\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)+\left\{-\left(\frac{1}{\theta}-1\right)|B|-1+\frac{d}{\theta}\right\}\log t

\displaystyle+t\left[\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{j\in A}x_{j}+\left\{\sum\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right\}^{\theta}\left\{1+O\left(e^{-\frac{t}{\theta}\min_{j\in A}x_{j}}\right)+O(t^{-1})\right\}\right]+o(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle tg(\bm{x})+(\log t)u_{1}(\bm{x})+u_{2}(\bm{x})

where, when letting
t\rightarrow\infty
, the gauge function is

\displaystyle g(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{j\in A}x_{j}+\left\{\sum\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right\}^{\theta}

the higher order terms are given by

\displaystyle u_{1}(\bm{x})=-\left(\frac{1}{\theta}-1\right)|B|-1+\frac{d}{\theta}

and

\displaystyle u_{2}(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle-\log\left[\frac{(-1)^{d+1}2^{-\frac{|A|}{\theta}}}{(\log 2)^{-2|C|}}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{d-1}\left(1{-}\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}\right]{-}\left(\frac{1}{\theta}{-}1\right)\sum\limits_{k\in B}\log(-x_{k}){-}(\theta-d)\log\left\{\sum\limits_{k\in B}\left(-x_{k}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right\}

There are 2 special cases to consider:

•

special case 1:
Suppose
x_{j}>0\;\forall\;j\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
and let
x_{(d)}=\min_{j=1,\dots,d}x_{j}
. Here, the joint log-density is

\displaystyle-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle-\log\left[2^{-1}(-1)^{d}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}\right]+t\left\{\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}+\left(1-\frac{d}{\theta}\right)x_{(d)}\right\}+

\displaystyle+2^{-1}e^{-tx_{(d)}}\left(1+o(1)\right)+o(1)=tg(\bm{x})+u_{1}(\bm{x})+o(1)

where the gauge function is

g(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}+\left(1-\frac{d}{\theta}\right)\min_{k=1,\dots,d}x_{k}

and the higher order term is

u_{1}(\bm{x})=-\log\left[2^{-1}(-1)^{d+1}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}\right],\qquad\text{as $t\rightarrow\infty$.}

•

special case 2:
Suppose
x_{j}<0\;\forall\;j\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
. Here, we have

\displaystyle-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle tg(\bm{x})+(\log t)u_{1}(\bm{x})+u_{2}(\bm{x})+o(1)

where the gauge function is

g(\bm{x})=\left\{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}\left(-x_{j}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right\}^{\theta}

the higher order terms are given by

u_{1}(\bm{x})=d-1

and

u_{2}(\bm{x})=-\log\left[(-1)^{d+1}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}\left\{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\left(-x_{j}\right)\right\}^{\frac{1}{\theta}-1}\left\{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}\left(-x_{j}\right)^{{1}/{\theta}}\right\}^{\theta-d}\right],\qquad\text{as $t\rightarrow\infty$.}

To study the case of
inverted logistic dependence
, first recall the definition of the joint distribution function in Fréchet margins

F_{F}(\bm{z})=\exp\left\{-V(\bm{z})\right\}

By evaluating at the univariate Frechet quantile function, get the Logistic copula

\displaystyle C_{\textit{log.}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{d})=

\displaystyle F_{F}\left(-\frac{1}{\log u_{1}},\dots,-\frac{1}{\log u_{d}}\right)=\exp\left\{-V\left(-\frac{1}{\log u_{1}},\dots,-\frac{1}{\log u_{d}}\right)\right\}

where
V
is specified to be the exponent function commonly associated with logistic dependence. The survival copula associated with the inverted logistic distribution is therefore given by

\displaystyle\bar{C}_{\textit{inv.log.}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{d})=

\displaystyle C_{\textit{log.}}(1-u_{1},\dots,1-u_{d})=\exp\left\{-V\left(-\frac{1}{\log(1-u_{1})},\dots,-\frac{1}{\log(1-u_{d})}\right)\right\}

So
x_{j}>0
(or
u_{j}>{1}/{2}
) in the logistic case corresponds to
x_{j}<0
(or
u_{j}<{1}/{2}
) in the inverted logistic case. This leads to the decomposition of the log-joint density in Laplace margins
-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=tg(\bm{x})+(\log t)u_{1}(\bm{x})+g^{\ast}_{0}(\bm{x})

where the gauge function is

\displaystyle g(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle\left(\sum\limits_{j\in A}x_{j}^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta}+\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{k\in B}(-x_{k})

the higher-order terms are given by

\displaystyle u_{1}(\bm{x})=-\left(\frac{1}{\theta}-1\right)|A|-1+\frac{d}{\theta}

and

\displaystyle u_{2}(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle-\log\left[(-1)^{d+1}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{d-1}\left(1{-}\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}2^{-\frac{|B|}{\theta}}(\log 2)^{-2|C|}\right]{-}\left(\frac{1}{\theta}{-}1\right)\sum\limits_{j\in A}\log(x_{j})-(\theta-d)\log\left(\sum\limits_{j\in A}x_{j}^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)

In the case where
x_{j}>0

\forall j\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
we have
-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=tg(\bm{x})+(\log t)u_{1}(\bm{x})+u_{2}(\bm{x})+o(1)

where the gauge function is

g(\bm{x})=\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta}

with higher-order terms given by

u_{1}(\bm{x})=d-1

and

u_{2}(\bm{x})=-\log\left[(-1)^{d+1}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}\left(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}\right)^{\frac{1}{\theta}-1}\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}^{{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta-d}\right]

as
t\rightarrow\infty
. In the case where
x_{j}<0

\forall j\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
we have

\displaystyle-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle tg(\bm{x})+u_{1}(\bm{x})+o(1)

where the gauge function is

g(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{\theta}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}(-x_{j})+\left(1-\frac{d}{\theta}\right)\min_{k=1,\dots,d}\left|x_{k}\right|

and the higher order term is

u_{1}(\bm{x})=-\log\left[2^{-1}(-1)^{d}\left\{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{\theta}\right)\right\}\right],\qquad\text{as $t\rightarrow\infty$}.

4.6
Multivariate Student-
t_{\nu}
copula, Student-
t_{\nu}
margins,
\nu>0

Suppose
\mathsf{Q}
positive definite and
\nu>0
.
The joint density can be expressed as
f(\bm{x})=f_{0}(g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x}))
,
where the homothetic function
f_{0}
and gauge function is fiven by

\displaystyle f_{0}(s)

\displaystyle=k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}(1+\nu^{-1}s^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\nu+d\right)},

\displaystyle g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}\right)^{{1}/{2}},

where
k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}={\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu+d}{2}\right)}/{\left\{\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu}{2}\right)\nu^{{d}/{2}}\pi^{{d}/{2}}\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|^{-{1}/{2}}\right\}}
. By the third condition of Proposition
6
, setting
\psi(t)=k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}(\nu+d)}t^{-(\nu+d)}
, and for
t>0
large, the higher-order term is

\displaystyle u(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{f(t\bm{x})}{\psi(t)}-g(\bm{x})^{-(\nu+d)}}{t^{-2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t^{2}\left[\psi(t)^{-1}k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}(\nu+d)}t^{-(\nu+d)}g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{-(\nu+d)}\left\{1+\nu t^{-2}g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{-2}\right\}^{-\frac{1}{2}(\nu+d)}-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{-(\nu+d)}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t^{2}\left[g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{-(\nu+d)}\left\{1-\frac{1}{2}(\nu+d)\nu t^{-2}g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{-2}+O(t^{-4})\right\}-g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{-(\nu+d)}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}(\nu+d)\nu g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{-2-(\nu+d)}+O(t^{-2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}(\nu+d)\nu g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{-2-(\nu+d)}+o(1)

4.7
Multivariate Student-
t_{\nu}
copula, Student-
t_{\nu}
margins,
\nu<0

Suppose
\mathsf{Q}
positive definite and
\nu<0
and
\nu<2-d

(
Papastathopoulos & Tawn 2013
)
.
For support
\left\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,1+\nu^{-1}\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}>0\right\}
, the joint density is given by

\displaystyle f(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}\left(1+\nu^{-1}\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\nu+d\right)}

where

k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}=\pi^{-{d}/{2}}\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|^{{1}/{2}}\left|\nu\right|^{1-\frac{d}{2}}\left(\left|\nu\right|-d\right)^{-1}\Gamma\left(\frac{\left|\nu\right|}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\left|\nu\right|-d}{2}\right)^{-1}
. Note the homothetic form
f(\bm{x})=f_{0}(g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x}))
, where

f_{0}(s)=k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}(1-s^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\nu+d\right)}

for
s\in(-1,1)
. Therefore, we extract the gauge function

g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})=\left(-\nu^{-1}\bm{x}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{x}\right)^{{1}/{2}}

Therefore,
r_{\mathcal{G}}(\cdot)=g_{\mathcal{G}}(\cdot)^{-1}
is a
-1
-homogeneous radial
function, and
r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})\bm{x}
lies on the boundary of the support
(i.e.,
f\left\{r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})\bm{x}\right\}=0
). Using
the transformation of the density function incondition in
Proposition
6

(i)
, we have the rate of convergence
black

\displaystyle f\left\{r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})\left(\bm{x}-t^{-1}\bm{1}\right)\right\}=

\displaystyle k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}\left[1-r_{\mathcal{G}}\left\{r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})\left(\bm{x}-t^{-1}\bm{1}\right)\right\}^{-2}\right]_{+}^{-\frac{1}{2}(\nu+d)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}\left[1-r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{2}r_{\mathcal{G}}\left(\bm{x}-t^{-1}\bm{1}\right)^{-2}\right]_{+}^{-\frac{1}{2}(\nu+d)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}\left[1-r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{2}\left\{r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})-\nabla r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})t^{-1}+O(t^{-2})\right\}^{-2}\right]_{+}^{-\frac{1}{2}(\nu+d)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}t^{\frac{1}{2}(\nu+d)}\left\{-\frac{2}{r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})}\nabla r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})+O(t^{-1})\right\}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{f\left\{r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})\left(\bm{x}-t^{-1}\bm{1}\right)\right\}}{k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}t^{\frac{1}{2}(\nu+d)}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})}\nabla r_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})+o(1)

4.8
Multivariate Student-
t_{\nu}
copula,
\nu>0
, Laplace margins

The multivariate t-distribution with positive definite precision
matrix
\mathsf{Q}=\left(q_{ij}\right)_{i,j=1}^{d}
and with
univariate t-distribution margins with
\nu
degrees of freedom is
given has joint density

\displaystyle f(\bm{z})=

\displaystyle k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\nu}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}q_{jj}z_{j}^{2}+\frac{2}{\nu}\sum\limits_{1\leq j<k\leq d}q_{jk}z_{j}z_{k}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\nu+d\right)}

where
k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}={\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu+d}{2}\right)}/{\left\{\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu}{2}\right)\nu^{{d}/{2}}\pi^{{d}/{2}}\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|^{-{1}/{2}}\right\}}
.
Perform the change of variables to the standard Laplace distribution, where we take advantage of a univariate t-distribution analoge of Mill’s ratio
(
Soms 1976
)
. Let
f_{t_{\nu}}
and
F_{t_{\nu}}
be the density and distribution functions of the univariate t-distribution with
\nu
degrees of freedom, respectively.

Suppose
z=z(tx)>0
(or
x>0
), then for
t>0
large, the change of variables from the t-distribution to the standard Laplace distribution

\displaystyle tx=

\displaystyle-\log\left[2\left\{1-F_{t_{\nu}}(z(tx))\right\}\right]=-\log\left[\frac{2\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu+1}{2}\right)\nu^{\left(\frac{1-\nu}{2}\right)}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu}{2}\right)\sqrt{\nu\pi}}\right]+\nu\log z(tx)+O\left(z(tx)^{-2}\right).

Inverting this transformation, obtain

\displaystyle z(tx)=

\displaystyle c_{\nu}e^{\frac{t}{\nu}x}\left(1+O\left(e^{\frac{-2t}{\nu}}\right)\right),

with partial derivative with respect to
tx
given by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dtx}z(tx)=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{\nu}}{\nu}e^{\frac{t}{\nu}x}\left(1+O\left(e^{\frac{-2t}{\nu}}\right)\right),

where
c_{\nu}=\left\{{2\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu+1}{2}\right)\nu^{\left(\frac{1-\nu}{2}\right)}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu}{2}\right)^{-1}{(\nu\pi)^{-{1}/{2}}}}\right\}^{{1}/{\nu}}
. When
z(tx)<0
(or
x<0
), the transformation to Laplace margins is

\displaystyle tx=

\displaystyle\log\left[2F_{t_{\nu}}(z(tx))\right]=\log 2+\log\left[1-F_{t_{\nu}}(-z(tx))\right].

Negating,

\displaystyle-tx=

\displaystyle-\log 2-\log\left(1-F_{t_{\nu}}(-z(tx))\right)=-\log\left[\frac{2\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu+1}{2}\right)\nu^{\left(\frac{1-\nu}{2}\right)}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu}{2}\right)\sqrt{\nu\pi}}\right]+\nu\log(-z(tx))+O\left(z(tx)^{-2}\right).

Inverting this transformation,

\displaystyle z(tx)=

\displaystyle-c_{\nu}e^{\frac{t}{\nu}\left|x\right|}\left(1+O\left(e^{\frac{-2t}{\nu}}\right)\right),

with partial derivative

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dtx}z(tx)=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{\nu}}{\nu}e^{\frac{t}{\nu}\left|x\right|}\left(1+O\left(e^{\frac{-2t}{\nu}}\right)\right).

Therefore, by change of variables, obtain the joint density in Laplace margins

\displaystyle f_{L}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle\left|\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\frac{d}{d(tx_{j})}z(tx_{j})\right|f(z(tx_{1}),\dots,z(tx_{d}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{\nu}^{d}k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}}{\nu^{d}}\exp\left\{\frac{t}{\nu}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}\left|x_{j}\right|\right\}\frac{q_{j^{\star}j^{\star}}c_{\nu}^{2}}{\nu}\exp\left\{-t\left(1+\frac{d}{\nu}\right)\max_{j=1,\dots,d}\left|x_{j}\right|\right\}(1+o(1)),

where
j^{\star}
is the index such that
\left|x_{j^{\star}}\right|=\max_{j=1,\dots,d}\left|x_{j}\right|
. Taking the negative logarithm, we obtain

\displaystyle-\log f_{L}(t\bm{x})=

\displaystyle tg(\bm{x})+u_{1}(\bm{x})+o(1),

where the gauge function is

\displaystyle g(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\nu}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}\left|x_{j}\right|+\left(1+\frac{d}{\nu}\right)\max_{j=1,\dots,d}\left|x_{j}\right|,

and the higher order term is

\displaystyle u_{1}(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle-\log\left(\frac{c_{\nu}^{d+2}k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}q_{j^{\star}j^{\star}}}{\nu^{d+1}}\right).

4.9
Wishart distribution

For a
d\times d
positive definite matrix
\bf{X}
, the Wishart
distribution with
\nu
degrees of freedom and positive definite scale
matrix
\bf{V}
has density function

f_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{X})=2^{-{(\nu d)}/{2}}\det(\mathbf{V})^{-{\nu}/{2}}\Gamma_{d}({\nu}/{2})^{-1}\det(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{1}{2}(\nu-d-1)}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\right)}

Thus,

-\log f_{\mathbf{X}}(t\mathbf{X})=tg(\mathbf{X})+(\log t)u_{1}(\mathbf{X})+u_{2}(\mathbf{X})

where

g(\mathbf{X})=\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\right)
is 1-homogeneous, and

u_{1}(\mathbf{X})=\left(-\frac{d}{2}(\nu-d-1)\right)
and

u_{2}(\mathbf{X})=-\frac{1}{2}(\nu-d-1)\log\det(\mathbf{X})+\log\left(2^{-{(\nu d)}{2}}\det(\mathbf{V})^{-{\nu}/{2}}\Gamma_{d}({\nu}/{2})^{-1}\right)

are higher-order terms.

Appendix 5
Directional densities

5.1
Multivariate normal distribution, standard normal margins

The joint density can be written in the form
f(\bm{x})=f_{0}(g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x}))
, where
f(s)=(2\pi)^{-{d}/{2}}\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|^{{1}/{2}}\exp\left\{-{s^{2}}/{2}\right\}
. By (
A.9
),

\displaystyle f_{\bm{W}}(\bm{w})=

\displaystyle g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x})^{-d}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}s^{d-1}f_{0}(s)ds=(2\pi)^{-{d}/{2}}\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|^{{1}/{2}}\left(\bm{w}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{w}\right)^{-{d}/{2}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}s^{d-1}e^{-\frac{1}{2}s^{2}}ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma({d}/{2})2^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(2\pi)^{-{d}/{2}}\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|^{{1}/{2}}\left(\bm{w}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{w}\right)^{-{d}/{2}}=\Gamma({d}/{2})2^{-1}\pi^{-{d}/{2}}\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|^{{1}/{2}}\left(\bm{w}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{w}\right)^{-{d}/{2}}

Figure 11
:
Directional density for the bivariate Gaussian distribution, standard Gaussian margins, with
\mathsf{Q}^{-1}_{11}=\mathsf{Q}^{-1}_{22}=1
,
\mathsf{Q}^{-1}_{12}=\mathsf{Q}^{-1}_{21}=0.8
, plotted over an empirical sample

5.2
Multivariate Laplace distribution, standard Laplace margins

We have
f_{[L]}(\bm{x})=f_{0}(g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x}))
, where
f_{0}(s)=|\mathsf{Q}|^{{1}/{2}}(2\pi)^{-{d}/{2}}s^{v}K_{v}(s)
. By (
A.9
), the density of angles is

\displaystyle f_{\bm{W}}(\bm{w})=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}r^{d-1}f_{0}(rg_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w}))dr=g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{-d}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}s^{d-1}f_{0}(s)ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\mathsf{Q}|^{{1}/{2}}(2\pi)^{-{d}/{2}}\left(\bm{w}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{w}\right)^{-{d}/{2}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}s^{d+v-1}K_{v}(s)ds=\Gamma({d}/{2})2^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(2\pi)^{-{d}/{2}}\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|^{{1}/{2}}\left(\bm{w}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{w}\right)^{-{d}/{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma({d}/{2})2^{-1}\pi^{-{d}/{2}}\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|^{{1}/{2}}\left(\bm{w}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{w}\right)^{-{d}/{2}}

where the expression for the integral can be found in Sections 6.5–6.7 of
Gradshteyn & Ryzhik 2014
.

Figure 12
:
Directional density for the bivariate Laplace distribution, standard Laplace margins, with
\mathsf{Q}^{-1}_{11}=\mathsf{Q}^{-1}_{22}=1
,
\mathsf{Q}^{-1}_{12}=\mathsf{Q}^{-1}_{21}=0.5
, plotted over an empirical sample

5.3
Multivariate Student
t_{\nu}
distribution, Student
t_{\nu}
margins,
\nu>0

We have
f(\bm{x})=f_{0}(g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{x}))
, where
f_{0}(s)=k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}\left(1+\nu^{-1}s^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\nu+d\right)}
. By (
A.9
), the density of angles is

\displaystyle f_{\bm{W}}(\bm{w})=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}r^{d-1}f_{0}(rg_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w}))dr=g_{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{w})^{-d}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}s^{d-1}f_{0}(s)ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{\nu,\mathsf{Q}}\left(\bm{w}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{w}\right)^{-{d}/{2}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}s^{d-1}\left(1+\nu^{-1}s^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\nu+d\right)}ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu+d}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu}{2}\right)^{-1}\nu^{-{d}/{2}}\pi^{-{d}/{2}}\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|^{{1}/{2}}\left(\bm{w}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{w}\right)^{-{d}/{2}}\nu^{-1+{d}/{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu+d}{2}\right)^{-1}\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\nu}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)2^{-{1}/{2}}\pi^{-{d}/{2}}\left|\mathsf{Q}\right|^{{1}/{2}}\left(\bm{w}^{\top}\mathsf{Q}\bm{w}\right)^{-{d}/{2}}

Figure 13
:
Directional density for the bivariate Student
t_{\nu}
distribution,
t_{\nu}
margins, with
\nu=5
and
\mathsf{Q}^{-1}_{11}=\mathsf{Q}^{-1}_{22}=1
,
\mathsf{Q}^{-1}_{12}=\mathsf{Q}^{-1}_{21}=0.6
, plotted over an empirical sample

5.4
Multivariate max-stable distribution, standard Fréchet margins

For a general
-1
-homogeneous exponent function,

\displaystyle f_{\bm{W}}(\bm{w})=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}r^{d-1}f(r\bm{w})dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}r^{d-1}\left[\sum\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}(-1)^{|\pi|}\prod_{s\in\pi}V_{s}(r\bm{w})\right]e^{-V(r\bm{w})}dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}(-1)^{|\pi|}\left[\prod_{s\in\pi}V_{s}(\bm{w})\right]\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}r^{d-\sum_{s\in\pi}(1+|s|)-1}e^{-r^{-1}V(\bm{w})}dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}(-1)^{|\pi|}\left[\prod_{s\in\pi}V_{s}(\bm{w})\right]\Gamma\left\{\sum_{s\in\pi}(1+|s|)-d\right\}V(\bm{w})^{\sum_{s\in\pi}(1+|s|)-d}

Suppose we fix
d=2
, and we assume we have logistic dependence and Fréchet margins, then the exponent function is given by
V(x,y)=(x^{-{1}/{\theta}}+y^{-{1}/{\theta}})^{\theta}
, and the true directional density
f_{\bm{W}}
can be plotted against an empirical sample of angles (see Figure
14
).

Figure 14
:
Directional density for the bivariate max-stable logistic distribution, standard Fréchet margins, with dependence parameter
\theta=0.3
, plotted over an empirical sample

5.5
Multivariate inverted max-stable distributions, standard exponential margins

Recall, from
subsection 4.4
that the first order gauge function for inverted max-stable distributions in exponential margins is given by the 1-homogeneous stable tail dependence function,
g(\bm{x})=\ell(\bm{x})
. With this in mind, the density of angles in this setting is given by

\displaystyle f_{\bm{W}}(\bm{w})=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}r^{d-1}f(r\bm{w})dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}r^{d-1}\left[\sum\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}(-1)^{|\pi|}\prod_{s\in\pi}\ell_{s}(r\bm{w})\right]e^{-\ell(r\bm{w})}dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}(-1)^{|\pi|}\left[\prod_{s\in\pi}\ell_{s}(\bm{w})\right]\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}r^{d+\sum_{s\in\pi}(1-|s|)-1}e^{-r\ell(\bm{w})}dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}(-1)^{|\pi|}\left[\prod_{s\in\pi}\ell_{s}(\bm{w})\right]\Gamma\left\{d+\sum_{s\in\pi}(1-|s|)\right\}\ell(\bm{w})^{d+\sum_{s\in\pi}(1-|s|)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\sum\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}(-1)^{|\pi|}\left[\prod_{s\in\pi}\ell_{s}(\bm{w})\right]\Gamma\left\{d+\sum_{s\in\pi}(1-|s|)\right\}g(\bm{w})^{\sum_{s\in\pi}(1-|s|)}\right]g(\bm{w})^{d},

thus following the expression given in equation (
36
) from Section
2.6
.
Suppose we fix
d=2
, and we assume we have logistic dependence and standard exponential margins, then the exponent function is given by
\ell(x,y)=(x^{{1}/{\theta}}+y^{{1}/{\theta}})^{\theta}
, and the true directional density
f_{\bm{W}}
can be plotted against an empirical sample of angles (see Figure
15
).

Figure 15
:
Directional density for the bivariate inverted max-stable logistic distribution, standard exponential margins, with dependence parameter
\theta=0.7
, plotted over an empirical sample

5.6
Wishart distribution

The density of angles is computed using

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{W}}(\mathbf{W})=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}r^{d-1}f_{\mathbf{X}}(r\mathbf{W})dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-{(\nu d)}/{2}}\det(\mathbf{V})^{-{\nu}/{2}}\Gamma_{d}({\nu}/{2})^{-1}\det(\mathbf{W})^{\frac{1}{2}(\nu-d-1)}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\left(1+\nu-d\right)\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{W}\right)\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}\left(1+\nu-d\right)}

where in this setting, radii and angles are defined using a matrix norm;
R=\|\mathbf{X}\|
,
\mathbf{W}={\mathbf{X}}/{\|\mathbf{X}\|}
.

Appendix 6
Statistical inference

6.1
Inference for latent variables

Given
n
independent
observations
\bm{y}:=\{r_{1}\bm{w}_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\bm{w}_{n}\}
from

\bm{X}=R\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the latent quantities of interest are the set

\mathcal{G}
, the quantile set
\mathcal{Q}_{q}
and the set
\mathcal{L}
. In what follows, we
detail the procedure to obtain realisations from the joint posterior
distribution of these quantities.

We model the logarithms of the random radial functions
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}
,

{r_{\mathcal{G}}}_{q}
and
r_{\mathcal{L}}
as Matérn (Gaussian) fields on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

using the stochastic partial differential equation (SPDE) approach by

Lindgren et al. 2011
, using
\alpha=2
(see their Equation (2)) which is
also the default option in the
R-INLA
package
(
www.r-inla.org
). We denote the respective random intercepts

\beta_{\mathcal{Q}},\beta_{\mathcal{G}},\beta_{\mathcal{L}}\in\mathbb{R}
as well as their stochastic
weights in the finite element representation

(
Lindgren et al. 2011
, Equation (9))
as

\bm{z}_{\mathcal{Q}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{G}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{L}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
We denote by
\bm{\theta}=(\beta_{\mathcal{Q}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{Q}},\beta_{\mathcal{G}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{G}},\beta_{\mathcal{L}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{L}})\in\mathbb{R}^{3p+3}
the set of all latent variables for our models, and it follows that the joint posterior distribution of
\bm{\theta}
fully determines that of
\mathcal{Q}_{q}
,
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{L}
. Due to the hierarchical structure of all proposed models detailed in Sections
3.2
and
3.3
, the joint posterior
density of
\bm{\theta}
factorises according to
\pi[\bm{\theta}\mid\bm{y}]=\pi[\beta_{\mathcal{G}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{G}},\beta_{\mathcal{L}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{L}}\mid\beta_{\mathcal{Q}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{Q}},\bm{y}]\pi[\beta_{\mathcal{Q}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{Q}}\mid\bm{y}]
. We first fit the Bayesian Gamma log-linear quantile regression model
described in Section
3.2
for a fixed probability
q

to all observations
\bm{y}
. Samples

\{(\beta_{\mathcal{Q},i},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{Q},i}):i=1,\ldots,n_{\mathcal{Q}}\}
from the posterior density

\pi[\beta_{\mathcal{Q}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{Q}}\mid\bm{y}]
map to a set of radial functions
\{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q},i}:i=1,\ldots,n_{\mathcal{Q}}\}
which are then interpreted as
candidate radial functions of a
q
-th quantile set. Given a radial
function
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q},i}
, we define a set of exceedances through

\mathcal{Y}_{i}=\left\{\left(r_{j},\bm{w}_{j}\right)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\;:\;r_{j}>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q},i}(\bm{w}_{j}),\,r_{j}\bm{w}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,j=1,\ldots,n\right\}.

(A.38)

We then fit model
\text{M}_{1}
,
\text{M}_{2}
, or
\text{M}_{3}
to each collection of
exceedances
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
, as detailed in Section
3.3
,
and also possibly include angles from non-exceedance data in the
likelihood. This procedure yields a conditional posterior density

\pi[\beta_{\mathcal{G}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{G}},\beta_{\mathcal{L}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{L}}\mid\beta_{\mathcal{Q},i},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{Q},i},\bm{y}]
for each
{i=1,\ldots,n_{\mathcal{Q}}}
. Sampling
n_{\mathcal{G}\mathcal{L}}
realisations jointly from each

\pi[\beta_{\mathcal{G}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{G}},\beta_{\mathcal{L}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{L}}\mid\beta_{\mathcal{Q},i},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{Q},i},\bm{y}]
provides an assembled sample of

n_{\mathcal{Q}}\cdot n_{\mathcal{G}\mathcal{L}}
realisations from the joint posterior
distribution of
\bm{\theta}
,

\left\{\bm{\theta}_{i,j}=\left(\beta_{\mathcal{Q},i},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{Q},i},\beta_{\mathcal{G},(i,j)},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{G},(i,j)},\beta_{\mathcal{L},(i,j)},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{L},(i,j)}\right)\in\bm{\Theta}\;:\;i=1,\ldots,n_{\mathcal{Q}},j=1,\ldots,n_{\mathcal{G}\mathcal{L}}\right\}.

(A.39)

For simplicity and without loss, we re-index the
sample (
A.39
) from the posterior distribution of

\bm{\theta}
to
\{\bm{\theta}_{i}\,:\,i=1,\ldots,n_{\bm{\theta}}\}

with
n_{\bm{\theta}}:=n_{\mathcal{Q}}\cdot n_{\mathcal{G}\mathcal{L}}
and use this notation
in the next sections. We shall also refer to the sampled latent
functions
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q},i}
,
r_{\mathcal{G},i}
, and
r_{\mathcal{L},i}
constructed from

\bm{\theta}_{i}
.

Interval estimation for the latent fields
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}
,
{r_{\mathcal{G}}}_{q}
, and
r_{\mathcal{L}}
is
accomplished via prediction
intervals
(
Bolin & Lindgren 2018
, see)
. A
(1-\alpha)

prediction-interval for the value of a random field

Y\,:\,\Omega\to\smash{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}}
at an angle
\bm{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

is the closed line segment

[q_{\alpha}(\bm{w})\bm{w}:q_{1-\alpha}(\bm{w})\bm{w}]

where
q_{\alpha}(\bm{w})
is the
\alpha
-quantile of the distribution
of
Y(\bm{w})
. A
(1-\alpha)
prediction interval for the process
Y

defined on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
consists of the strip

R_{1-\alpha}:=\cup_{\bm{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}R_{1-\alpha}(\bm{w})
defined through
R_{1-\alpha}(\bm{w}):=[q_{\rho}(\bm{w})\bm{w}:q_{1-\rho}(\bm{w})\bm{w}]

for some
\rho
such that
q_{\rho}(\bm{w})
and
q_{1-\rho}(\bm{w})
satisfy

\mathbb{P}\left[q_{\rho}(\bm{w})\leq Y(\bm{w})\leq q_{1-\rho}(\bm{w}),\bm{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\right]=1-\alpha.

In the context of our latent fields
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}
,
{r_{\mathcal{G}}}_{1}
, and
r_{\mathcal{L}}
,

(1-\alpha)
prediction intervals consist of sets withing within which
the true functions lie entirely with probability
1-\alpha
. Prediction intervals can be obtained from a sample from the posterior
distribution of a parameter of interest using the
excursions

package in
\{+1\}
.
(
Bolin & Lindgren 2015
,
Bolin & Lindgren 2017
)
.

6.2
Rare event probability estimation

Given
n
independent observations

\bm{y}:=\{r_{1}\bm{w}_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\bm{w}_{n}\}
from

\bm{X}=R\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, interest lies in the estimation of the
probability that a new draw from
R\bm{W}
falls within some Borel set

B\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
far enough from the origin (
\bm{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
). We provide a statistical inference framework for

{\mathbb{P}_{B\mid\bm{y}}:=\mathbb{P}[R\bm{W}\in B\mid\bm{y}]}
. In practice, common
types of sets of interest are boxes

\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\bm{a}\leq\bm{x}\leq\bm{b},\,\bm{a},\bm{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
and sets of the form
\{r\bm{w}:r>h(\bm{w})>0,r\in(0,\infty),\bm{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\}
for some positive function
h
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, both
starshaped at
\bm{0}
. This is useful in our setting as it allows for an exact probability
calculation with respect to our model specification for exceedances. Simple adaptations can be made to consider more complex sets.

For a set
B\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
starshaped at
\bm{0}
, define
S_{B}:=\{\bm{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}:r\bm{w}\in B,r\in(0,\infty]\}\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and consider, for any
\bm{w}\in S_{B}
, the
partition
I_{\inf}(\bm{w})\cup I_{B}(\bm{w})\cup I_{\sup}(\bm{w})
of

(0,\infty)
, where

I_{\inf}(\bm{w}):=(0,r_{B_{\inf}}(\bm{w}))
,

I_{B}(\bm{w}):=[r_{B_{\inf}}(\bm{w}),r_{B}(\bm{w})]
, and

I_{\sup}(\bm{w}):=(r_{B}(\bm{w}),\infty)
for the radial function

r_{B}(\bm{w})
of
B
and the function
r_{B_{\inf}}(\bm{w})=\inf\{r>0:r\bm{w}\in B\}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{B\mid\bm{y}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[R\in I_{B}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}\in S_{B}\mid\bm{y}],

(A.40)

The posterior predictive distribution in (
A.40
) is given from the posterior density of
\bm{\theta}
via

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{B\mid\bm{y}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3p+3}}\mathbb{P}[R\in I_{B}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}\in S_{B}\mid\bm{\theta}]\pi[\bm{\theta}\mid\bm{y}]\,d\bm{\theta},

(A.41)

where
\pi[\bm{\theta}\mid\bm{y}]
is obtained following the procedure described in Section
6.1
. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{q}
is a latent variable,
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})
intersects with
I_{\inf}(\bm{w})
,
I_{B}(\bm{w})
, or
I_{\sup}(\bm{w})
with non-zero probability for all
\bm{w}\in S_{B}
. Hence, from total probability,
\mathbb{P}_{B\mid\bm{\theta}}:=\mathbb{P}[R\in I_{B}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}\in S_{B}\mid\bm{\theta}]
of (
A.41
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{B\mid\bm{\theta}}=\mathbb{P}[R\bm{W}\in B,R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W})\mid\bm{\theta}]+\mathbb{P}[R\bm{W}\in B,R\leq r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W})\mid\bm{\theta}],

(A.42)

and our model is best suited for sets
B
such that the second term in the
equation (
A.42
) is small. The first term in equation (
A.42
) decomposes into

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[R\bm{W}\in B,R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W})\mid\bm{\theta}]=\mathbb{P}[R\in I_{B}(\bm{W})\mid\bm{W}\in S_{B},R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W}),\bm{\theta}]\times

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\;\mathbb{P}[\bm{W}\in S_{B}\mid R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W}),\bm{\theta}]\mathbb{P}[R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W})\mid\bm{\theta}].\ \quad

(A.43)

By the assumptions detailed in Section
3.2
, the last term
\mathbb{P}[R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W})\mid\bm{\theta}]
in
equation (
6.2
) equals
1-q
for all
\bm{\theta}\sim\pi[\bm{\theta}\mid\bm{y}]
. Following our model formulation for
\bm{W}
, the second term in
equation (
6.2
) corresponds to the predictive
distribution of the angles given
\bm{\theta}
and is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\bm{W}\in S_{B}\mid R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W}),\bm{\theta}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{S_{B}}\widetilde{f}_{\bm{W}\mid\bm{\theta}}(\bm{w}\mid\bm{\theta})d\bm{w}\bigg/\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\widetilde{f}_{\bm{W}\mid\bm{\theta}}(\bm{w}\mid\bm{\theta})d\bm{w},\quad

(A.44)

where
\widetilde{f}_{\bm{W}}
is chosen from
\text{M}_{1}
,
\text{M}_{2}
, or
\text{M}_{3}

(see Section
2.6
). The integrals in (
A.44
) are computed
efficiently via numerical integration for
d=2
or
3
. The first
term in (
6.2
) is obtained through

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[R\in I_{B}(\bm{W})\mid\bm{W}\in S_{B},R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W}),\bm{\theta}]=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{W}\mid\bm{W}\in S_{B},\bm{y}}(\mathbb{P}[R\in I_{B}(\bm{W})\mid R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W}),\bm{\theta}]),\quad~~

(A.45)

where
\mathbb{P}[R\in I_{B}(\bm{w})\mid\bm{W}=\bm{w},R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w}),\bm{\theta}]
, needed for the expectation calculation in equation (
A.45
), equals

\displaystyle F_{R_{E}\mid\bm{W}}\left[\frac{\max\left\{r_{B}(\bm{w}),r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})\right\}-r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}{{r_{\mathcal{G}}}_{q}(\bm{w})}\bigg\lvert\bm{w}\right]-F_{R_{E}\mid\bm{W}}\left[\frac{\max\left\{r_{B_{\inf}}(\bm{w}),r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})\right\}-r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{w})}{{r_{\mathcal{G}}}_{q}(\bm{w})}\bigg\lvert\bm{w}\right],\quad

(A.46)

with
F_{R_{E}\mid\bm{W}}(z\mid\bm{w})=1-[1+\xi(\bm{w})z]_{+}^{-1/\xi(\bm{w})}
. We approximate
expectation (
A.45
) through Monte-Carlo integration by
sampling angles

\{\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{n_{w}}\}
from

f_{\bm{W}}
restricted to the set
S_{B}
via

\displaystyle n_{w}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{w}}\mathbb{P}[R\in I_{B}(\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{i})\mid R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{i}),\bm{\theta}]

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[R\in I_{B}(\bm{W})\mid\bm{W}\in S_{B},R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W}),\bm{\theta}],

(A.47)

as
n_{w}\to\infty
. The second term in equation (
A.42
),

\mathbb{P}[R\bm{W}\in B,R\leq r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W})\mid\bm{\theta}]=:p
,
corresponds to a probability over a region in which our model is not
specified. We hence consider it as the success probability of the binomial random variable describing the number of observations
falling in the set
\mathcal{Q}_{q}\cap B
. For each

i\in\{1,\ldots,n_{\bm{\theta}}\}
, we impose a binomial likelihood and
beta prior for the parameter
p
, leading to a closed form beta
posterior distribution. When
\mathcal{Q}_{q}\cap B\neq\varnothing
, we
sample a probability from the posterior distribution of
p
and add it
accordingly in equation (
A.42
).

In practice, inference for
\mathbb{P}_{B\mid\bm{y}}
is performed by first collecting a sample
\{\bm{\theta}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\theta}_{n_{\bm{\theta}}}\}
from
\pi[\bm{\theta}\mid\bm{y}]
through the procedure described in Section
6.1
, and then calculating
\mathbb{P}_{B\mid\bm{\theta}_{i}}
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n_{\bm{\theta}}\}
.
A posteriori
mean estimates corresponding to the Monte-Carlo integration

\displaystyle n_{\bm{\theta}}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\bm{\theta}}}\mathbb{P}_{B\mid\bm{\theta}_{i}}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{B\mid\bm{y}},\quad\text{as }n_{\bm{\theta}}\to\infty,

(A.48)

approximating equation (
A.41
). Equal-tailed credible intervals from the sample
\{\mathbb{P}_{B\mid\bm{\theta}_{i}}:i=1,\ldots,n_{\theta}\}
hence complete the inference procedure for
\mathbb{P}_{B\mid\bm{y}}
.

6.3
Model selection and validation

Our model formulation gives rise to various modelling choices needing
to be assessed and validated. For instance, as discussed in
Section
3.3
, selecting the most appropriate model
within the set of candidate models
\text{M}_{1}
,
\text{M}_{2}
, and
\text{M}_{3}
given
observed data amounts to analysing the properties of the distribution
of
R,\bm{W}\mid R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}(\bm{W})
with respect to the association
between
g_{\mathcal{G}}
and
f_{\bm{W}}
. Another modelling choice requiring
assessment is that of the set of angles contributing to
likelihood (
51
) since it translates into a
bias-variance trade-off for
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{L}
. Further, hyper-parameters
values imposed on the prior distributions of
\mathcal{Q}_{q}
,
\mathcal{G}
, and
\mathcal{L}

imply
a priori
information on the differentiability
properties of their boundaries (see Section
6.1
), and
their impact on the posterior may need assessment. Finally, the usual
concern of the sensitivity of the posterior distribution of the latent
variables to the return period for the latent threshold function

r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}
(Section
3.2
) remains. We detail possible methods
for model selection and validation below.

The nested structure of model
\text{M}_{2}

within the parameter space of model
\text{M}_{3}
raises the question of
whether model
\text{M}_{2}
can serve as a sensible simplification of model

\text{M}_{3}
given observed data

\bm{y}=\{r_{1}\bm{w}_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\bm{w}_{n}\}
. As discussed in
Section
3.3
, evidence for a constant
r_{\mathcal{B}}
in model
\text{M}_{3}
points to evidence that
\text{M}_{2}
can serve as an
sensible simplification of
\text{M}_{3}
, or equivalently that the observed
data
\bm{y}
may come from a process following a homothetic density with respect to its gauge function
g_{\mathcal{G}}
. Given a sample of random functions
\{\psi(\bm{w})^{\top}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{B},i}:i=1,\ldots,n_{\theta}\}

from the posterior distribution of the latent random field

\psi(\bm{w})^{\top}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{B}}
, we obtain a
0.95

prediction interval,
R_{0.95}
, for
\psi(\bm{W})^{\top}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{B}}
through the procedure described in Section
6.1
. We say there is evidence against a homothetic underlying desnity whenever there is some non-empty subset
S_{0}\subset\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
such that
0\notin R_{0.95}(\bm{w})
for
\bm{w}\in S_{0}
.

The sensitivity of the posterior distributions of the latent
parameters to the remaining modelling choices discussed in the
beginning of this section can be assessed through the quality of the
calibration of the posterior predictive distribution to the observed
data. Given a set of observed exceedances
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
of a sampled
latent function
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q},i}
(see expression (
A.38
)), we
wish to assess the calibration of the predictive distribution for the
excess variable
R-r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q},i}(\bm{W})\mid R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q},i}(\bm{W})
. We thus
compare each observed excess
r_{j}-r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q},i}(\bm{w}_{j})
with the
quantile of the Exponential
({r_{\mathcal{G}}}_{q}(\bm{w}_{j}))
, with

\log{r_{\mathcal{G}}}_{q}(\bm{w})=\beta_{\mathcal{G}}+\bm{\psi}(\bm{w})^{\top}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{G}}
and

(\beta_{\mathcal{G}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{G}})\sim\pi[\beta_{\mathcal{G}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{G}}\mid\beta_{\mathcal{Q},i},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{Q},i},\bm{y}]
. We draw exceedance
radii
\{\widetilde{r}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{r}_{n_{s}}\}
from their
predictive distribution Exp
({r_{\mathcal{G}}}_{q}(\bm{w}_{j}))
, and define the empirical
distribution function

F_{\bm{w}_{j},n_{s}}(r):={n_{s}}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{s}}\mathbbm{1}[\widetilde{r}_{k}\leq r]
, for
r\in(0,\infty)
. A probability-probability (PP)
plot for the exceedances
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
is then given by

\displaystyle\left\{\left(\frac{j}{\lvert\mathcal{Y}_{i}\rvert+1},p_{(j)}\right):j=1,\ldots,\lvert\mathcal{Y}_{i}|\right\},

(A.49)

where
p_{(j)}
denotes the
j
-th order statistic of the sample
\{F_{\bm{w}_{j},n_{s}}(r_{j}-r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q},i}(\bm{w}_{j})):j=1,\ldots,\lvert\mathcal{Y}_{i}|\}
. A quantile-quantile (QQ) plot in unit exponential margins for the exceedances
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
is then easily obtained via
probability-probability (PP) plot for the exceedances
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
via

\displaystyle\left\{\left(-\log\left(1-\frac{j}{\lvert\mathcal{Y}_{i}\rvert+1}\right),-\log\left(1-p_{(j)}\right)\right):j=1,\ldots,\lvert\mathcal{Y}_{i}|\right\}.

(A.50)

In a similar fashion, we can obtain PP plots for the predictive
distribution of
\bm{W}\mid R>r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q},i}(\bm{W})
. This is achieved by
sampling angles

\{\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{n_{s}}\}
from the
predictive density

f_{\bm{W}\mid\beta_{\mathcal{G}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{G}},\beta_{\mathcal{B}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{B}},\bm{y}}

where

(\beta_{\mathcal{G}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{G}},\beta_{\mathcal{B}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{B}})\sim\pi[\beta_{\mathcal{G}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{G}},\beta_{\mathcal{B}},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{B}}\mid\beta_{\mathcal{Q},i},\bm{z}_{\mathcal{Q},i},\bm{y}]
. We transform the sampled angles and the observed
angles from
\mathcal{Y}
to spherical coordinates, respectively denoted

\{\widetilde{\bm{\varphi}}_{j}=(\widetilde{\varphi}_{1,j},\ldots,\widetilde{\varphi}_{d-1,j})\in\Phi:j=1,\ldots,n_{s}\}

and

\{\bm{\varphi}_{j}=(\varphi_{1,j},\ldots,\varphi_{d-1,j})\in\Phi:j=1,\ldots,\lvert\mathcal{Y}_{i}\rvert\}
,
and consider the empirical distribution function

F_{n_{s}}(\bm{\varphi}):={n_{s}}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{s}}\mathbbm{1}[\widetilde{\bm{\varphi}}_{k}\leq\bm{\varphi}]
. A PP plot for the calibration of the
predictive distribution of angles to the observed angles in

\mathcal{Y}_{i}
is then given by expression (
A.49
) with

p_{(j)}
denoting the
j
-th order statistic of the sample

\{F_{n_{s}}(\bm{\varphi}_{j}):j=1,\ldots,\lvert\mathcal{Y}_{i}|\}
.

Appendix 7
Simulation study

7.1
Setting

We here describe the setting of a simulation study performed using
samples from classical parametric bivariate copulas. We provide a
brief summary of the properties of these
distributions.

A random variable
\bm{X}_{L}
is said to follow a bivariate Laplace
distribution with precision matrix
\mathsf{Q}=\mathsf{\Sigma}^{-1}

for a positive definite covariance
\mathsf{\Sigma}
—with unit
diagonal terms and a correlation value
\rho\in[-1,1]
on the
remaining off-diagonal termsThe parameter
\rho
hence fully determines a member of the family of
the bivariate normal distribution as well as its extremal properties
and in the subsequent sections, we use
\rho=0.5
.

A random vector
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
has a bivariate normal distribution
with Laplace marginal distributions and precision matrix

\mathsf{Q}=\Sigma^{-1}
for a positive definite covariance

\mathsf{\Sigma}
—with unit diagonal terms and a correlation value

\rho\in[-1,1]
on the remaining off-diagonal
terms. In what follows, we use the bivariate normal distribution as an
instance of asymptotically independent distribution and draw samples
using the value
\rho=0.8
.

A random vector
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
has a bivariate max-stable logistic
distribution with Laplace margins parametrised by an exponent function

V(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}^{-{1}/{\theta}}\right)^{\theta}
where the dependence parameter
\theta\in(0,1]
. The max-stable
logistic distribution belongs the class of asymptotic dependent
distributions provided
\theta\neq 1
. For any
\theta\in(0,1)
,

\alpha_{\mid i}=1
and
\eta=1
. We carry the simulation study using

\theta=0.3
. Table
1
presents, for each
family of distributions, the values of extremal coefficients
associated with fixed parameters.

Extremal coefficient

Laplace

Normal

Max-stable logistic

\alpha_{\mid i}

\rho=0.5

\rho^{2}=0.64

1

\eta

\sqrt{(1-\rho^{2})/(2-2\rho)}\approx 0.866

(1+\rho)/2=0.9

1

Table 1
:
Values of the extremal coefficients
\alpha_{\mid i}
and
\eta
for the bivariate Laplace, normal, and max-stable logistic distributions with specified parameters.

7.2
Posterior distribution of latent variables

We perform a simulation study to obtain realisations from the
posterior distribution of the latent sets
\mathcal{Q}_{q}
,

\mathcal{L}
, and
\mathcal{G}
, as well as from the posterior predictive distribution
of
R
and
\bm{W}
. We centre the simulation study on the
bivariate Laplace, normal, and max-stable logistic families of
distributions. The bivariate Laplace distribution is chosen for the
homothetic structure of its density with respect to its gauge function
g_{\mathcal{G}}
, implying
that its marginal density of the angles is exactly that assumed by model

\text{M}_{2}
. The bivariate max-stable logistic and normal distributions are
chosen respectively for their asymptotic dependence and independence properties. From each of these distributions, we
generate
100
samples of size
n_{1}=1000
and
100
samples of size

n_{2}=10000
, resulting in six cases (600 samples) with which we carry
our analyses.

Figure 16
:

0.95
prediction intervals for

draws from a

(right) distributions in Laplace margins. Points correspond to
exceedances of at least one sampled posterior

r_{\mathcal{Q}_{0.8}}
.

Figure 17
:

of
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}
for
n
draws from the bivariate Laplace
(left), normal (centre), max-stable logistic (right)
distributions in Laplace margins. Within panels:
n_{1}
,

1-q_{1}=0.2
(left),
n_{2}
,
1-q_{2}=0.05
(right). Red segments:
values of
1-q_{k}
used in the fitting
procedure.

Following the posterior sampling procedure described in
Section
6.1
, we first fit a bayesian Gamma quantile
regression to each of the
600
samples described above using

q_{1}=0.8
for each sample of size
n_{1}
and
q_{2}=0.98
for each
samples of size
n_{2}
. Here,
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
are chosen such that the
expected number of exceedances of
\mathcal{Q}_{q_{k}}
,

n_{k}\cdot\mathbb{P}[R\bm{W}\in\mathcal{Q}_{q_{k}}^{\prime}\mid\bm{y}]
, equals
200
for

{k=\{1,2\}}
. Again for each sample, we obtain
20
realisations
\{r_{\mathcal{Q}_{{q_{k}},i}}:i=1,\ldots,20\}

from the posterior distribution of
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q_{k}}}
and define
20

sets of exceedances accordingly. Figure
17
displays the
angle-wise posterior mean and
0.95
prediction intervals
for
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{0.8}}
resulting from fitting quantile regressions to
one sample of
n_{1}
draws from each of the three distributions of
interest. Figure
17
also displays the data points defined
as exceedances for at least one realisation from the posterior
distribution of
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{0.8}}
. A brief assessment of the
performance of the quantile regression is made through
Figure
17
, which consists of boxplots of the
number of exceedances of each realisation from the posterior
distribution of
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q_{k}}}
for each of the
600
samples
described above grouped by family of distributions and sample size.

Figure 18
:

\{\varphi_{j}\in[0,2\pi]:\bm{w}_{j}=(\cos\varphi_{j},\sin\varphi_{j}),j=1,\ldots,n_{k}\}

, and the true density

\mathcal{Q}_{0.98}
only (sample size
n_{2}
). Fourth column:
\pi[\bm{W}\mid\mathcal{G},\mathcal{L},\bm{y}]
fitted using
all sampled angles (sample size
n_{2}
).

Given the satisfying properties of the posterior distribution of

r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q_{k}}}
, we proceed with the investigation of the joint model
for
R
and
\bm{W}
. Following the procedure described in
Section
6.1
, we obtain realisations from the
posterior distributions of
r_{\mathcal{G}}
and
r_{\mathcal{L}}
—say

\{r_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}:i=1,\ldots,n_{\theta}\}
and

\{r_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}:i=1,\ldots,n_{\theta}\}
for

n_{\theta}=n_{\mathcal{Q}}n_{\mathcal{G}\mathcal{L}}=20\cdot 50
—using each model
\text{M}_{1}
,
\text{M}_{2}
,
and
\text{M}_{3}
on each sample described above. Due to
expression (
29
), we consider the
two cases of including only the angles of exceedances of the sampled
quantile sets
\mathcal{Q}_{q_{k},i}
and including all observed angles

(\bm{w}_{1},\ldots,\bm{w}_{n})
in the model likelihood. Figure
18
provides a comparison of the posterior
predictive density of angles
\pi[\bm{W}\mid\mathcal{G},\mathcal{L},\bm{y}]
output by
models
\text{M}_{1}
,
\text{M}_{2}
, and
\text{M}_{3}
fitted on samples of sizes
n_{1}
and

n_{2}
from a multivariate Laplace distribution in Laplace margins. As
discussed in Section
2.6
, the marginal density of
angles for the multivariate Laplace distribution is known exactly
given the homothetic structure of its density with respect to its
gauge function
g_{\mathcal{G}}
. This allows for validation of the performance
of
\text{M}_{1}
,
\text{M}_{2}
, and
\text{M}_{3}
. It is clear from
Figure
18
that all three models recover the true or the
empirical marginal density of angles of the multivariate Laplace
distribution well. As expected, including all angles in the fitting
procedure and increasing the sample size both lead to reduction in the
uncertainty of the posterior predictive density

\pi[\bm{W}\mid\mathcal{G},\mathcal{L},\bm{y}]
.

Figure 19
:
Histogram of the angles

\{\varphi_{j}\in[0,2\pi]:\bm{w}_{j}=(\cos\varphi_{j},\sin\varphi_{j}),j=1,\ldots,n_{2}\}
of
n_{2}
draws from a
bivariate max-stable logistic distribution, with angle-wise
posterior mean (black line) and
0.95
prediction intervals
(grey) for the marginal distribution of angles

\pi[\bm{W}\mid\mathcal{G},\mathcal{L},\bm{y}]
. From left to right: outputs of

\text{M}_{1}
,
\text{M}_{2}
, and
\text{M}_{3}
.

Figure 20
:
Estimated limit sets from
n_{2}
draws from a scaled
bivariate max-stable logistic distribution, with posterior mean
(black line),
0.95
prediction interval (grey), and true
limit set (red line). From left to right: outputs of
\text{M}_{1}
,

\text{M}_{2}
, and
\text{M}_{3}
.

The max-stable logistic distribution is an instance of family of
distributions for which
\text{M}_{2}
is known to be a wrong model for the
marginal density of the angles. However, it is observed from
Figure
20
—which displays the mean of the posterior
predictive density
\pi[\bm{W}\mid\mathcal{G},\mathcal{L},\bm{y}]
as well as its
0.95

prediction interval—that all three models possess the ability to
capture the empirical distribution of angles when incorporating all
sampled angles in the model likelihood. Figure
20
depicts some consequences of this. When the number of data used to fit
\pi[\bm{W}\mid\mathcal{G},\mathcal{L},\bm{y}]
is
greater than the number of exceedances of
\mathcal{Q}_{q_{k}}
, the posterior
distribution of the gauge function
g_{\mathcal{G}}
obtained from
\text{M}_{2}
is
allowed to over-fit to the directional component of the model. This
generates bias and over-confidence in the posterior distribution of
the gauge function
g_{\mathcal{G}}
and of its associated limit boundary

\partial\mathcal{G}
. Under
\text{M}_{1}
, the decoupling of the dependence of the
density of angles
\pi[\bm{W}\mid\mathcal{G},\mathcal{L},\bm{y}]
on the gauge function

g_{\mathcal{G}}
(so that
\pi[\bm{W}\mid\mathcal{G},\mathcal{L},\bm{y}]=\pi[\bm{W}\mid\bm{y}]
) is
expected to remove potential bias in
g_{\mathcal{G}}
coming from the observed
angles. A clear consequence, however, is the increase in the variance
of the posterior distribution of
g_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Figure
21

provides, for each of the modelling choices detailed in the beginning of this Section, a boxplot of the mean of
the mean integrated square distance (MISD) from each realisation of
the posterior of
\partial\mathcal{G}
. Model
\text{M}_{3}
, in all cases, seems to be the best—or approximately as
valid as the best—model in terms of MISD.

Figure 21
:
Boxplots of the means of the mean integrated squared error
(MISE) from each posterior realisations of
\partial\mathcal{G}
from
each of the
600
samples to the true limit sets. From top to

n_{1}
,
n_{2}
. From left to right columns:

,

, and

using only angles of exceedances
(light grey) and using all angles (dark

Figure
22
presents the angle-wise mean of
the posterior distribution of the limit boundaries
\partial\mathcal{G}

estimated from
\text{M}_{3}
with the associated
0.95
prediction
interval. As expected, increasing the sample size decreases uncertainty
in the estimated limit boundaries. One may also infer that when it is
reasonable to assume that observed extremes come from the same data
generating mechanism as all other observations, including all sampled
angles into the likelihood provides smoother estimates of
\partial\mathcal{G}

than a procedure using only the exceedances of
\mathcal{Q}_{q_{k}}
. We argue that while there may be valid reasons to select
\text{M}_{1}
or

\text{M}_{2}
—respectively for evidence of a homothetic data generating
mechanism or of a poor matching between the density of angles

\pi[\bm{W}\mid\mathcal{G},\mathcal{L},\bm{y}]
and the homothetic density
\mathcal{G}^{d}/\lvert\mathcal{G}^{d}\rvert
—,
\text{M}_{3}

generally provides a better-behaved and more balanced choice.

),

(sample size
n_{2}
).

7.3
Estimating rare event probabilities

Based on the results of Figures
21

and
22
, we carry the remainder of this
simulation study under the framework of model
\text{M}_{3}
and use all
observed angles for the cases of the bivariate Laplace and max-stable
logistic distributions and only those of exceedances of
\mathcal{Q}_{q_{k}}
for the bivariate normal distribution. For each
of the
600
samples defined in Section
7.2
,
we obtain realisations from the posterior distribution of

\mathbb{P}_{B_{i}\mid\bm{y}}
according to the procedure described in
Section
6.1
to estimate the probability that a new
observation
R\bm{W}
falls within extreme sets
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
, and
B_{3}

as defined in Figure
23
. Figure
24

displays boxplots of the log-mean of the realisations from the
posterior predictive distribution of each set
B_{i}
and each sample.

Figure 23
:
Regions
B_{1}=[11,13]\times[11,13]

for
probability estimation superposed with samples of size
n_{1}

for the Gaussian (left), logistic (centre), and multivariate

margins.

It is clear that the mean of predictive posterior distribution is
well-behaved for the sets
B_{i}
corresponding to directional subsets of

\mathbb{S}
where the posterior distribution of the limit sets presented in
Section
7.2
is itself well-behaved. Table
2
details the coverage obtained from

100
samples for the probability that a new observation
R\bm{W}

falls within the sets
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
, and
B_{3}
based on
0.95
prediction
intervals based on the quantile method for the posterior predictive
distributions. The coverage properties of the prediction intervals seem
to be satisfying, except for the set
B_{2}
where the true probability
is on the order of
10^{-16}
and
10^{-14}
for the bivariate normal
and max-stable logistic distributions respectively. We again note
that these correspond to regions in which the posterior distributions
of
r_{\mathcal{G}}
are biased in the case of the bivariate
normal distribution. We argue that reasonable coverage properties are
obtained in other cases.

Appendix 8
Case studies

8.1
Posterior model fits on river data

On the bivariate Thames tributary data, we fit models using the three architectures
\text{M}_{1}
,
\text{M}_{2}
, and
\text{M}_{3}
using data exceeding a high posterior quantile estimate and on all available data. Figures
29
and
30
show posterior mean limit set boundary and posterior directional densities for each of the six fitted models. In these figures, we see that the fitted model associated with the
\text{M}_{2}
architecture and fitted on exceedance data only is best. This model’s posterior limit set agrees most with
\log n
-scaled data, while the posterior mean directional density is in good agreement with an empirical sample of angles. The posterior QQ plots in Figure
31
corresponding to this optimal model shows good agreement with the radial exceedance model and the underlying exponential distribution, while the PP plots in
32
show general agreement between the posterior and the underlying directional models.

8.2
Additional Newlyn wave height figures

On the three-dimensional Newlyn wave data, we fit models using the
three architectures
\text{M}_{1}
,
\text{M}_{2}
, and
\text{M}_{3}
using data
exceeding a high posterior quantile estimate and on all available
data. Figure
33
show posterior mean
limit set boundaries for each of the six fitted models. In these
figures, we see that the fitted model associated with the
\text{M}_{2}

architecture and fitted on exceedance data only agrees most with

\log n
-scaled data, though all models fit well. The posterior QQ
plots in Figure
34
corresponding to this optimal model
shows good agreement with the radial exceedance model and the
underlying exponential distribution, while the PP plots in

35
show good agreement between the posterior and
the underlying directional models. In Section
4
of the main
body of this manuscript we see that this model is also very accurate
in estimating other diagnostics. One diagnostic not mentioned in
Section
4
of the main body is plots of
\chi_{q}(A)
,
defined in equation (
1
) in Section
1
of
the main body. Figure
36
shows posterior mean
estimates of
\chi_{q}(A)
for
A\in\left\{HP,HS,PS,HPS\right\}
and
for
q\in(0.9,1)
with posterior 95% confidence intervals.
Wadsworth & Campbell 2024
establish that the variable groups

HP
,
PS
, and
HPS
are asymptotically independent, and that
HS
is
asymptotically dependent. With this in mind, we conclude that our
posterior joint model is able to accurately describe the extremal
dependence structure presented in this dataset. Figure
36
correctly shows posterior estimates of

\chi_{q}(A)
tending to 0 as
q
tends to
1
for the asymptotically
independent groups, and tending to a nonzero value close to the
empirical estimate for the asymptotically dependent pair
HS
.

(a)

\text{M}_{1}
, exc. only

(b)

\text{M}_{1}
, all angles

(c)

\text{M}_{2}
, exc. only

(d)

\text{M}_{2}
, all angles

(e)

\text{M}_{3}
, exc. only

(f)

\text{M}_{3}
, all angles

Appendix 9
Bivariate Gaussian mixture model fits

Here, we recreate the task of return level set boundary estimation in
the case of bivariate Gaussian mixture data presented in Section 4 of

Hallin et al. 2021
. Since our approach is non-empirical,
we are able to extrapolate to higher levels than observed in the data
and introduce uncertainty in our estimates. In
Figure
37
, the return level curves, along with
0.95 credible intervals, are presented for return periods
T=20
(or

q=0.95
) and
T=10^{5}
(or
q=1-10^{-5}
). For this each mixture
distribution, we generated
n=5,000
datapoints and fit a quantile
regression model with threshold at the
q=0.9
level. For the joint
model, we used the
\text{M}_{3}
hierarchical structure. For display
purposes, 20 posterior observations of
r_{\mathcal{Q}_{q}}
were drawn and a
joint radial-directional model was fit at each of these observations. There were 50 draws of posterior return level curves drawn for each of
these models for a total of 1,000 return level curves for which to
obtain posterior mean and credible intervals for each plot in
Figure
37
.

Figure 37
:
Posterior return level set boundaries for return periods
T=20
(inner) and
T=10^{5}
(outer) for 3 different bivariate Gaussian mixture distributions. Grey points are the data in original margins, solid black lines indicate the posterior mean return level set boundaries, with dark grey regions indicating the corresponding 0.95 credible bands, bordered by dotted lines.
